alternative approach to this “emergent” symmetry structure in eikonal QNMs see [227].
The nature of the simplifications involved perhaps necessitates that some effective
emergent symmetry governs the eikonal QNM spectrum. It is less obvious that this
symmetry should be realized in terms of local spacetime isometries, which reflects that,
while QNMs are ordinarily sensitive to the global spacetime structure, eikonal QNMs
become localized at the light-ring.
Another instance where a subset of QNMs organizes according to a “spacetime-limit”
isometry are the ZDMs of near-extremal rotating BHs (see also Section (2.1.5)). These
QNMs take the form [12, 44, 46, 81]
ωℓmn ≈mΩH −i(n + hℓm)2πTH ,
(2.48)
where TH →0 is the BH temperature, ΩH →1/2M is the horizon angular velocity, and
Re(hℓm) = 1/2 for modes with m ∼ℓ[47].
The modes (2.48) are closely related
to interesting phenomenology near-extremal rotating BHs, such as a “polynomial
ringdown” phase [47, 77, 78, 261, 262].
They organize themselves (approximately)
into representations of the SL(2, R) × U(1) isometry of the near-horizon, near-extremal
limit metric [263, 264], a warped version of the limiting AdS2 × S2 (AdSp+2 × Sd−p−2)
near-horizon of extremal charged BHs (p-branes) [265].
The Penrose limit can capture the eikonal limit of Eq. (2.48) as (near-equatorial)
corotating bound null geodesics go into the near-horizon region [266]: see [243, 267] for


=====PAGE 44=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
44
the interplay between the near-horizon and the Penrose limit descriptions. Notably, the
isometries of a spacetime are a “hereditary property”; any limit spacetime will have at
least as much isometries as its parent [259, 268, 269]. Nevertheless, the nature of the
isometries changes through a group contraction [270], which is clearly illustrated in the
interplay between the near-horizon and Penrose limits [271]. For the interpretation of the
near-horizon Penrose limit within the context of the AdS/CFT conjecture, see [272, 273].
As discussed in detail in Section 2.6, even “small” changes can drastically alter the
QNM spectrum. The connection between the light ring and the eikonal QNMs can
similarly be broken. For instance, if an effective cavity forms – either because of exotic
physics at the horizon or because the BH is immersed in some environment – waves
can go back and forth between the light ring and the other features involved, such as
AdS asymptotics or the surface of an exotic compact star (see also Section 3.3). The
argument that null congruences around the light ring are the only ones consistent with
the QNM boundary conditions no longer holds, and the direct relation of the light ring
with the eikonal QNMs is broken. Alternatively, the leading eikonal equation can be
modified such that null geodesic congruences no longer provide the relevant geometrical
optics limit. Given that the eikonal equation is determined by the “principal symbol”
of a PDE, the terms with the maximal number of differential operators, this routinely
happens in higher-derivative theories [274].
The remainder of the review indicates that observational opportunities are pushing
for an understanding of the ringdown in more quantitative detail. This comes with
computational challenges and results that are not always easy to interpret. The eikonal
limit can provide helpful clarifications. However, to do so, it too must be developed
further. The Penrose limit perspective has natural (Penrose-Güven limit) extensions to
include additional fields [275] which would for instance be applicable to the Kerr-Newman
case (see Section 2.2). The limiting plane waves are almost universal solutions to higher-
derivative gravity theories in which they are naturally incorporated [276]: see Section 3.1
for potential applications. The Penrose limit also may also lead to a better understanding
of qualitative aspects of nonlinearities (see Sections 4.3 and 4.4 and Refs. [277–280]
for preliminary work in this direction). Higher-order Fermi null coordinate expansions
could help relate higher-order eikonal results directly to curvature properties around the
light-ring [254]. Finally, it is precisely the nearly bound null geodesics that are responsible
for a distinctive “photon ring” in BH images [281, 282]. Current Event Horizon Telescope
images are not yet able to resolve the universal, accretion-independent features of this
photon ring, but future observations have the potential to do so [283, 284]. For this
reason, the eikonal limit of QNMs is a direct link between GW and very-long-baseline
interferometry observations of BHs.
2.5. Mode scalar products: completeness, orthogonality, and other properties
Initial contributors: Green, London, Panosso Macedo, Sberna


=====PAGE 45=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
45
2.5.1. Introduction
Perturbed BHs are inherently nonconservative systems, as they
generally emit gravitational radiation that can escape to infinity or fall into the BH
horizon. This means that to understand the properties of their QNM spectrum – in
mathematical terms, of QNMs as a kind of vector space – we cannot rely on the familiar
theory of conservative systems. In conservative systems (e.g., a particle in a box in
quantum mechanics), the equations of motion are Hermitian, their spectrum consists of
real eigenvalues, and the related eigenfunctions are referred to as normal modes [285–287].
Normal modes are orthogonal with respect to an inner product and constitute a complete
set. These properties can be exploited to build general solutions, to compute mode
excitation coefficients, and to build perturbative solutions to more complex problems.
They also ensure the stability of the spectrum [194].
Nonconservative (or non-Hermitian) problems are not unique to gravity, but rather
they are ubiquitous in physics, from electromagnetism to nuclear physics, in either
classical or quantum systems [193]. It is therefore not surprising that the first scalar
product for BH QNMs, appearing as far back as 1993, was inspired by QNMs in optical
cavities [204, 288] (see also [289, 290] for reviews). The scalar product introduced there
was a bilinear form for Schwarzschild QNMs. Here, a distinction needs to be made
between inner products (commonly used in conservative systems) and bilinear forms. An
inner product is a map from a vector space to a field that is (i) conjugate-symmetric, i.e.,
linear in one argument and conjugate linear in the other; and (ii) positive definite. An
example of an inner product in quantum mechanics is the integral ⟨f, g⟩=
R b
a f(x)g(x)dx
between two continuous, complex-valued functions on the interval [a, b]. A bilinear form
is also a map from a vector space to a field, but one that is linear in both arguments and
is not guaranteed to be positive definite. Such a Schwarzschild QNM bilinear form was
connected to the QNM excitation coefficients [204], and it was used to compute frequency
shifts due to a perturbation in the potential of the mode equation [205, 207]. This
approach was later extended to Kerr, and applied to compute perturbative corrections
to the Kerr frequencies [77, 102, 208, 209]. Later, motivated by the practicalities of GW
signal modeling, situations in which the Kerr QNM spheroidal harmonics are complete
have been pointed out [40], and this motivates special functions which enable QNM
orthogonality over the solid angle [291–294]. More recently, a product under which the
Kerr QNMs display full spatial orthogonality was presented [295].
A notable technical complication in the definition of Kerr QNM products arises from
the asymptotic behavior of QNMs at the bifurcation surface and spatial infinity, located
at r = r+ and r →∞, respectively, in the Boyer-Lindquist coordinates. The QNM
radial eigenfunctions diverge exponentially in these two limits, so inner products must be
modified to give finite results (this same issue is encountered in other physical systems
with QNMs, such as resonators in electromagnetism [296]). The simplest resolution is
to compute the inner product by deforming the radial integration into regions of the
complex plane where modes decay exponentially [155, 295, 297, 298]. An example of such
a contour in the Boyer-Lindquist radial coordinate, for modes with Re(ω1 + ω2) > 0, is
given in Fig. 11. In certain cases, convergence may be accomplished also by subtracting


=====PAGE 46=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
46
r
r+
r−
𝒞
Figure 11. Integration contour for QNM products (C, solid black line) in the Boyer-
Lindquist coordinate r. We also show the branch cuts of the radial function, stemming
from r± (dashed red).
appropriate boundary terms: see e.g. [154, 204, 210, 288, 299]. Alternatively, it has
long been suggested [300, 301] that selecting suitable hypersurfaces, correctly foliating
both the BH horizon and future null infinity, should yield QNM regular functions
asymptotically. The hyperboloidal framework in BH perturbation theory, discussed in
Section 2.5.4 below, exploits this geometrical strategy. By constructing time-translation
invariant hyperboloidal coordinates in BH spacetimes [302] and identifying their relation
to regularization schemes in the FD [66, 303], the framework has become a widespread
approach in BH perturbation theory over the past decades (see [304, 305] for recent
reviews). Besides addressing the asymptotic divergence of QNMs, the hyperboloidal
framework offers a powerful approach to go beyond the linear regime and to properly
resolve late-time tails (see Sections 4.4, 4.5, and 5.3).
This body of work, reviewed in more detail in the following subsections, demonstrated
that some properties of conservative systems can be recovered – at least to some extent,
and at the cost of additional mathematical complexity. There is indeed a sense in which
Kerr QNMs are vectors within a topological space and orthogonal. Simple extensions of
time-independent perturbation theory can be used to compute QNM frequency shifts. We
will review a number of these product spaces for QNMs, as summarized in Table 3. Scalar
products of QNMs have been used in several applications, including the calculation of
perturbative frequency shifts [102, 210, 306], the study of avoided crossings and resonances
in Kerr QNMs (see Section 2.3.3), and the modeling of GWs from BBHs [294, 307].
Other properties of conservative systems, such as spectral stability (see Section 2.6) and
completeness (discussed briefly here and in Section 4.1), are unfortunately lost for Kerr
QNM spectra. For this reason we will also review some recent developments in the field
achieved by importing tools from the theory of non-normal operators [308, 309] to gravity
via the hyperboloidal framework, including the notion of pseudospectrum [308, 310, 311]
and the so-called Keldysh projection scheme [312].
In the subsections that follow we will pay considerable attention to the spatial
properties of Kerr QNMs, in particular their spatial orthogonality [40, 295, 313], as well

Primer: QNMs as a vector space in the azimuthal variable ϕ. We will begin by considering
the azimuthal dependence eimϕ in Eq. (2.3). This provides an unambiguous yet restrictive
avenue to better understand QNM orthogonality and completeness. It is also a reference
relative to which some nontrivial aspects of QNM vector spaces may be framed.
The requirement that QNM eigenfunctions be continuous and smooth as one rotates
around the BH spin direction (i.e., along ϕ) means that m may only take on integer
values. Therefore, for fixed s, ℓand n, the QNMs constitute a countably infinite set
whose natural label or order is m ∈Z. The related complex exponential functions, eimϕ,

different sets. This is characteristic of the scalar products encountered throughout this
section, and reflects the complex nature of the QNM frequencies. In Eq. (2.49b), Iϕ is
simply the identity operator, meaning that if Ψ(ϕ) is an arbitrary function of ϕ with
domain [0, 2π], then
Iϕ |Ψ⟩=
∞
X
m=−∞
|ψm⟩⟨ψ∗
m | Ψ⟩ϕ .
(2.50)
Together, Eqs. (2.49) and (2.50) define a topological vector space, specifically: a set of
vectors, ψm, and a way to compute distances between them with ⟨· | ·⟩ϕ. We will refer to
this as the ϕ scalar product space, or Qϕ, where
Qϕ = {⟨· | ·⟩ϕ, Uϕ}
(2.51)
In Eq. (2.51), we have defined Uϕ = {eimϕ | m ∈Z}. Since Uϕ is complete, and norms
may be defined via ⟨· | ·⟩ϕ, Qϕ is a Hilbert space.
In summary, the azimuthal functions used in QNM solutions are orthogonal and
complete, and can thereby serve as a basis for general decomposition. Indeed, these
functions are widely used throughout GW theory (see e.g. [314, 315]). Note that, since
Eqs. (2.49) and (2.50) are independent of the QNM frequency, they apply to all QNM
eigenfunctions equally. In the following, we will discuss how QNMs may share these
properties, either as full spatial functions, or in single spatial dimensions. Historically,
these possibilities have been most commonly studied using Sturm-Liouville theory.
2.5.2. Sturm-Liouville theory in the Teukolsky formalism
Given Teukolsky’s master
equation, one can apply ideas from Sturm-Liouville theory to construct scalar-product
spaces for the QNMs. These spaces provide a foundation for understanding whether it is
possible to use the QNMs for projection and decomposition, much as is done with special
functions in electromagnetism and quantum mechanics. Key to this program is the
intersection between Sturm-Liouville theory and complex operator theory [42, 316, 317].
According to Sturm-Liouville theory, a multi- or uni-variate ordinary differential
operator may be used to (i) construct a scalar product under which the operator is


=====PAGE 49=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
49
self-adjoint, (ii) combine the operator’s eigenfunctions with the scalar product to define a
vector space, and (iii) study the properties of that scalar product space in the context of
physical applications. These applications include (but are not limited to) the estimation
of separation constants, the approximation of QNM angular and radial functions, and
potential “spectral decomposition” with QNMs, for which it is required that the QNMs be
spatially complete in some way. The points above apply to the full spatial dependence of
the QNMs, as well as to their angular and radial functions, sSℓ(θ; ˜ωℓmn) and sRℓ(θ; ˜ωℓmn),
respectively. Therefore, it is possible to construct a scalar product space over (r, θ, ϕ),
as well as spaces over r, θ and ϕ separately. We have already encountered the scalar
product space in ϕ, Qϕ. Like Qϕ, the remaining spaces are defined by a scalar product,
as well as a set of functions (abstract vectors) which span the space.
In this subsection, we will briefly review the construction and properties of select
Sturm-Liouville scalar product spaces, namely Qθ and Qrθ [40, 208, 295]. The reader
should note that, since Teukolsky’s master equation is an operator with complex
coefficients, it is possible to have degenerate eigenvalues, as well as related Jordan
chains [318, 319]. As it has been observed that this is not the case for Schwarzschild BHs
and for Kerr BHs below the extremal limit, we will henceforth focus only on cases in which
Teukolsky’s radial and angular problems have unique eigenvalues [40, 42, 43]. Lastly, in
what follows we will include within Sturm-Liouville theory two types of situations: one
in which we consider a single QNM frequency (“single-frequency” case), and another in
which we consider two QNM frequencies concurrently (“two-frequency” case). These two
cases are built upon slightly different versions of Sturm-Liouville arguments [285, 286].
Sturm-Liouville theory and indefinite scalar products. Let Ttrθϕ be the linear differential
operator corresponding to Teukolsky’s equation in Boyer-Lindquist coordinates, as shown
in Eq. (2.2). With a QNM frequency ω in mind, we wish to consider the action of
Ttrθϕ on functions whose t and ϕ dependence is defined by an overall proportionality to
e−i(ωt−mϕ), as in Eq. (2.3). Since the result will still be proportional to e−i(ωt−mϕ), it is
useful to consider a new operator, Trθ,
Trθ = ei(ωt−mϕ) Ttrθϕ e−i(ωt−mϕ) I .
(2.52)
On the right-hand side of Eq. (2.52), Ttrθϕ acts on the quantity (e−i(ωt−mϕ) I), where I
(the identity operator) stands in for an arbitrary function of r and θ. Sturm-Liouville
theory provides a simple framework for the construction of scalar products. Since Trθ
involves derivatives scaled by complex-valued functions, related scalar products will
typically be nonpositive definite or, equivalently, indefinite [320, 321].
The key idea is that one may attempt to define a weight function, W, such that
WTrθ is formally self-adjoint:
⟨f | WTrθ g⟩= ⟨WTrθ f | g⟩,
(2.53)
where ⟨· | ·⟩refers to an integral over a domain volume. Note that formally self-adjoint
operators may not satisfy Sturm-Liouville boundary conditions [286]. Any second order


=====PAGE 50=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
50
linear operator may be formatted by coordinate and/or similarity transformation to be
formally self-adjoint; however, the required transformations may involve impractical or
nonalgebraic functions [286, 287]. One then redefines the scalar product to implicitly
include the weight function, making the operator self-adjoint with respect to the newly
defined product [286]. As demonstrated in many texts [139, 285–287, 322–324], the
property of self-adjointness imposes constraints on an operator’s form, and has the
simplifying benefit of requiring that an operator’s matrix elements are symmetric in a
suitably chosen vector space. All linear differential operators may be reformatted (via
coordinate transformation and/or appropriate scaling of a weight function) such they
are formally self-adjoint.
Single-frequency Sturm-Liouville weights. For example, let us consider the polar space,
Qθ. Using Sturm-Liouville theory, we will construct a polar scalar product, ⟨· | ·⟩θ, and
then briefly discuss the radial scalar product. According to Sturm-Liouville theory, ⟨· | ·⟩θ
is defined such that Trθ is self-adjoint by first rewriting it as
Trθ = p0(r, θ, ∂r) + p1(θ) ∂
∂θ + p2(θ) ∂2
∂θ2 .
(2.54)
In Eq. (2.54), the functions p0, p1 and p2 are simply the coefficients of derivatives with
respect to θ. Their explicit form may be determined by inspection. Since there are no
cross-derivatives between r and θ, we have grouped derivatives in r (i.e., ∂r) into p0.
Following standard Sturm-Liouville arguments [286, 287], a weight Wθ for which WθTrθ
is self-adjoint is
Wθ =

Wr = ∆(r)s = (r −r+)s (r −r−)s .
(2.56)
Recall that r+ and r−are defined below Eq. (2.1). Equations (2.55) and (2.56) may also
be derived by considering Teukolsky’s radial and angular equations separately.
Sturm-Liouville weights depend strongly on coordinate and scaling choices.
Coordinate choices affect the weight function via the chain rule applied to the scalar
product’s integrand [42]. Choices about spacetime-slicing affect the spatial asymptotic
behavior of the QNMs, and are therefore interchangeable with different similarity
transformations, akin to Eq. (2.52) [35, 42, 325]. These similarity transformations
are sometimes referred to as scaling or “s-homotopic” transformations [41, 42, 326]. In
the context of Frobenius-series solutions to the QNM problem, these transformations
correspond to solutions with different spatial asymptotic behaviors [42, 43, 327].


=====PAGE 51=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
51
The spatial behavior of QNM solutions is constrained by their phase velocity
near the BH horizon and spatial infinity [15].
Therefore, while one may consider
arbitrary similarity transformations on the QNM problem, the underlying physics is
only consistent with transformations that preserve the QNM ingoing-outgoing boundary
conditions [15, 42, 48, 52].
For example, Wr has been used in the context of operator perturbation theory for
QNMs [208, 297]; however, as it fails to incorporate the QNM asymptotic boundary
conditions, this weight is not the most appropriate for studying the potential spatial
completeness of QNMs [42, 43]. Upon imposing the QNM boundary conditions [42], Wr
transforms into a complex valued Pollaczek-Jacobi weight [328, 329],
Wξ = ξB0 (1 −ξ)B1 e
B2
1−ξ
(2.57)
where ξ = (r −r+)/(r −r−), and B0 through B2 are defined in terms of a, s, M
and ω [42]. Polynomials derived from Wξ enable one to express Teukolsky’s radial
operator for QNMs as a symmetric tridiagonal matrix, and Eq. (2.5) as a simple matrix
eigenvalue problem [43]. These observations point to intersections between QNMs, matrix
representations of Sturm-Liouville operators, and the study of orthogonal polynomial
systems [40, 42, 43, 316, 330, 331]. In the case of the spheroidal harmonics for QNMs,
these intersections are one avenue through which QNM spatial completeness may be
studied [40, 294].
The Sturm-Liouville weights defined in Eqs. (2.55) and (2.56) have a few important
properties that are relevant to their potential use in defining a unique projection operator,
similar to Iϕ in Eq. (2.50). Both Wr and Wθ are independent of the QNM frequency; as
a result, the related scalar products could be applied equally to different QNMs that
make up GW signals. Concurrently, Wr and Wθ were derived using Trθ which is, by
construction, frequency-dependent [42]: see Eq. (2.2). This is illustrated by the fact that
Wr can be similarity-transformed into the explicitly frequency-dependent weight Wξ,
without affecting the underlying physics. Thus, similarity transformations enable the
conversion of frequency-agnostic Sturm-Liouville weights into single-frequency weights,
and vice-versa.
Two-frequency weights, and the 2D space Qrθ. Using a variant of the Sturm-Liouville
construction [285], two-frequency weights may be constructed from the action of the
(r, θ) part of Teukolsky’s operator on two different QNM solutions [154, 295, 297]. The
(r, θ) part of Teukolsky’s operator, Trθ, was shown in Eq. (2.52). Here, it is useful to
refer to this operator as Trθ(w), to explicitly indicate its dependence on a single QNM
frequency. Following the logic of Eq. (2.55a), Trθ(ω) is self-adjoint with respect to the
2D product whose weight is Wr Wθ, and whose domain of integration is the BH exterior.
For two arbitrary functions, f(r, θ) and g(r, θ), this product is

The polar space, Qθ. An alternative perspective [40, 294] concludes that decomposition
is possible with the QNM spheroidal harmonics, but that it requires a generalization
of orthogonality called biorthogonality (i.e., orthogonality between members of two
different sets), as well as the use of special functions called adjoint-spheroidal harmonics.
The resulting decomposition can be applied, for example, to extreme and comparable
mass-ratio BBHs [294]. Below, we briefly review the treatment of the QNM spheroidal
harmonics as a Hilbert space. In what follows, we will drop the labels of s, m and n,
as they will all be considered fixed, and we will refer only to the θ-dependence of the
spherical and spheroidal harmonics; e.g., Sℓ= sSℓ(θ; aωℓmn).
For fixed values of s, m and n, the following properties hold [40]: (i) the QNM
angular separation constants are unique, even for very large values of ℓ, and (ii) there
is a close relationship between the spherical and spheroidal harmonics, meaning that
there exists a one-to-one mapping between them. The related spherical-spheroidal
mixing coefficients may be used to construct an invertible operator, T, that converts
spin-weighted spherical harmonics into the QNM spheroidal harmonics,
T =

means that Qθ is a Hilbert space.
2.5.3. QNM orthogonality product.
A bilinear form with respect to which the time-
translation operator is symmetric can be constructed to show that QNMs of the spin-s
Teukolsky equation are orthogonal [295]. This construction, inspired by the physics
of leaky optical cavities [334] and other open systems, and already applied to scalar
waves in Schwarzschild [204, 205, 207, 288], provides a more general derivation of the
orthogonality of QNMs.
The starting point is a conserved current defined by the Teukolsky operator. Here
the Teukolsky operator acting on spin-s solutions Ψ [the left-hand side of Eq. (2.3)] is
denoted by O. The current also involves the spacetime adjoint of the Teukolsky equation,
denoted by O† and acting on spin −s solutions ˜Ψ (we denote this “spacetime adjoint” to
stress the fact, often left implicit in the literature, that the product defining the adjoint
is the spacetime integral). The current πa is a functional of a spin s and a spin −s
solution, and is implicitly defined by
(O† ˜ψ)ψ −˜ψ(Oψ) = ∇aπa[ ˜ψ, ψ].
(2.70)
The current is evidently conserved on solutions, i.e., ∇aπa[ ˜ψ, ψ] = 0 for ψ ∈ker O, ˜ψ ∈
ker O†. A “base product” can then be built as the associated conserved quantity on any
spacelike hypersurface,
Π[ ˜ψ, ψ] =
Z
dΣaπa[ ˜ψ, ψ].
(2.71)
To build a product on two modes of the same spin s, one can map a mode of spin s
into a mode of spin −s using the operator J associated to the discrete t −ϕ reflection
symmetry of Kerr [295]. The resulting product is
⟨⟨ψ1, ψ2⟩⟩=
Z

the following key properties [295]: (i) it does not rely on the spatial separability
of the Teukolsky equation; (ii) it is bilinear (a bilinear form); (iii) it is symmetric,
⟨⟨ψ1, ψ2⟩⟩= ⟨⟨ψ2, ψ1⟩⟩. Moreover, the time-translation symmetry operator Lt of Kerr is
symmetric with respect to the product, ⟨⟨ψ1, Ltψ2⟩⟩= ⟨⟨Ltψ1, ψ2⟩⟩. Consequently, for
two QNMs, (ω1 −ω2)⟨⟨ψ1, ψ2⟩⟩= 0, implying that modes with different frequencies are
orthogonal. Equivalently, {ψn} and { ˜ψn ≡Ψ2s/3

2.5.4.
The hyperboloidal framework, the energy product, and the pseudospectrum
The study of scalar products was initially motivated by attempts to understand the
completeness properties of the QNM eigenfunctions [337, 338]. As previously mentioned,
however, the radial integration used in all of the products introduced so far requires a
regularization of the QNM eigenfunctions via a deformation of the relevant integration
contour in the complex r plane, as in Fig. 11. Over the past decades, the so-called
hyperboloidal framework [66, 302–305] has become a widespread geometrical approach
in BH perturbation theory to avoid the divergence of QNMs on spacelike hypersurfaces
near the bifurcation sphere and spatial infinity.
In Riemannian geometry, spherical coordinates (or their spheroidal deformations)
play a crucial role to capture symmetries of the manifold or to simplify the equations.
Hyperboloids play a similar role in Lorentzian geometries [339], as they capture key
features of the spacetime’s casual structure. In the particular case of BH spacetimes, the
level sets of constant hyperboloidal time coordinate are regular spacelike hypersurfaces,


=====PAGE 56=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
56
extending smoothly from the BH event horizon to future null infinity. Most importantly,
they reveal that QNM eigenfunctions are regular in the BH exterior [66, 340–346].
In essence, a hyperboloidal foliation ¯xµ = (τ, σ, θ, φ) follows from the usual Boyer-
Lindquist coordinates xµ = (t, r, θ, ϕ) via
t = r+

τ −H(σ)

,
r = r0 + r+ −r0
σ
,
ϕ = φ −ϕ∗(r).
(2.75)
Each element in Eq. (2.75) contributes to the regularity of the QNM eigenfunctions at
the BH horizon and at future null infinity in different ways. For instance, the azimuthal
angle transformation defined by the relation dϕ∗/dr = a/∆is well-known for the ingoing
Kerr coordinates, and it ensures the regularity of observables across the BH horizon.
To properly obtain a hyperboloidal surface, the height function H(σ) in Eq. (2.75)
must satisfy specific asymptotic conditions [302–304]. The so-called minimal gauge
retains just these minimal conditions when constructing a hyperboloidal foliation, and
H(σ) reads [304, 347]
H(σ) = −1 −r0/r+

The radial compactification in Eq. (2.75) includes only the minimal structure
necessary to bring the infinitely far wave zone into a finite range of coordinate values.
Indeed, σ = 0 represents future null infinity, whereas the choice in Eq. (2.75) conveniently
fixes the BH horizon r = r+ to σ+ = 1. This minimal compactification is not unique,
as it allows for an arbitrary initial offset r0 in the radial coordinate. In terms of this
parameter, the Cauchy horizon at r = r−is mapped into σ−= (r+ −r0)/(r−−r0).
This arbitrary constant offers some freedom to understand and explore the Kerr
spacetime extremal limit from this geometrical perspective [347]. A straightforward
option is to choose r0 = 0, which implies σ−= r+/r−, i.e., the coordinate value of
the Cauchy horizon depends on the BH rotation parameter a. As a consequence, the
hyperboloidal hypersurfaces τ = constant foliate the Cauchy horizon smoothly, and the
extremal Kerr limit is directly achieved when |a| →M and r−→r+ [325, 347]. This
option is equivalent to the hyperboloidal implementation used in [348].
A second option is to fix the Cauchy horizon at a coordinate value independent of
the BH rotation parameter. The choice r0 = r−, for instance, is such that σ−→∞for
all values of a. In this way, the hypersurfaces τ = constant do not cross the Cauchy
horizon [325], and the extremal limit provides a discontinuous transition into the Kerr
near-horizon geometry [347].
This option is the spacetime counterpart of Leaver’s
strategy [66, 326, 347, 349, 350].
The construction of Eqs. (2.75),(2.76) automatically yields a configuration where
outgoing boundary conditions at both the BH horizon and the wave zone are satisfied.
The physically relevant scenario is described by the regular solution to the resulting

!
.
(2.77)
The second-order differential operator L1 assumes a singular Sturm–Liouville form, and
the first-order differential operator L2 incorporates the energy flow into the BH and
towards I + [310, 351].
An early objective of this approach was to utilize the fact that QNMs defined on
hyperboloidal surfaces “are represented by proper eigenvalues and eigenfunctions” [301].
A FD transformation⃗u ∼esτ⃗u leads to the eigenvalue problem ˆL⃗u = s⃗u, with the
dimensionless parameter s = −ir+ω. Consequently, the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
ˆL correspond directly to the QNM eigenfrequencies and eigenfunctions [310]. Advances
in this framework for spherically symmetric spacetimes have been significant [304, 310],
with successful applications to the Kerr spacetime [347, 348].
The regularity of the QNM eigenfunctions allows one to revisit the completeness
properties of the QNM eigenfunctions [337, 338] via the representation of solutions as a
complete spectral decomposition in terms of a superposition of a discrete spectrum (the
QNMs) and of a continuous spectrum from the branch cut contribution [66, 350]:
¯ϕℓm(τ, σ) =
∞
X
n=0
ηnℓm ¯ϕnℓm(σ)esnℓmτ +
Z
Re(s)≤0
ηℓm(ω)esτ ¯ϕnℓm(s; σ)dω.
(2.78)
The notation ¯ϕ emphasizes that these fields are defined in terms of the hyperboloidal
coordinate system, with ¯ϕnℓm(σ) the regular QNM eigenfunctions. An extension of
Leaver’s algorithm [66, 350] allows one to define QNMs as the poles of a discrete Green’s
function (see e.g. Eq. (73) in [66]) and to compute the QNM and branch cut amplitudes,
ηn and η(s), from a given set of initial data via a discrete projection operator (see e.g.
Eqs. (106) and (109) in [66]). The orthogonality of QNMs is verified in terms of these
discrete projection operators. The spectral decomposition of Eq. (2.78) has been recently
revisited in terms of the so-called Keldysh scheme [290, 312, 351], that extends the
analysis in [66] beyond one-dimensional problems. This scheme relies on defining the
QNM projection solely in terms of quantities in the dual vector space [312], in particular
not requiring a scalar product. Proof-of-principle studies indicate that the transpose
operator Lt provides the elements to express Eq. (2.78) with QNM/tail amplitudes fixed
by projections of the initial data into the dual vector space [312].
The early studies on the completeness properties of the QNM eigenfunctions [337,
338] also noticed a peculiar feature: the QNM spectrum changed drastically when
considering a discrete approximation to the Regge-Wheeler potential. In particular,
the (continuum representation) QNMs were not recovered, even in the limit where the
discrete representation was pushed closer to the continuous counterpart. Since this FD
instability did not seem to directly affect the TD signal at moderately early times, the


=====PAGE 58=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
58
issue was regarded as a mostly technical curiosity. However, spectral instability is now
understood to be a broad property of BH spectra which occurs quite generically in the
presence of environmental perturbations (see [352, 353] for early work, and Section 2.6
for a more extensive discussion). The issue of spectral instabilities – possibly introduced
by dilute astrophysical environments or even corrections to Einstein’s theory – is of
paramount importance. We discuss it more thoroughly below in Section 2.6, but note
that detection strategies and systematic effects, discussed in Section 6.1.7, are directly
impacted by such possible instabilities.
The development of a hyperboloidal framework was a crucial tool in our
understanding of these instabilities as it also enabled the application of non-self-adjoint
operator theory [308, 309] to gravitational studies [310]. In particular, the notion of
pseudospectrum [308] provides a formal explanation for QNM spectral instability, and it
facilitates a nonmodal analysis [311]. The ϵ-pseudospectrum ςϵ(ω) is defined as the set
of points in the complex frequency plane such that
ςϵ(s) ≡



ˆL −s
−1

E
> 1/ϵ.
(2.79)
The limit ϵ →0 recovers the spectrum of the operator ˆL. For ϵ ≳0, the pseudospectrum
reveals the spectral properties of the operator as a topographic map on the complex plane,
with peaks located at the complex eigenvalues. Unlike the spectrum, the computation of
the pseudospectrum requires a well-defined notion of norm to distinguish “large” from
“small” ϵ values. A norm reflecting the field’s energy on a constant-time hypersurface,
based on the scalar product of the field and its derivatives, is given schematically
by [310, 351]
⟨¯ϕ| ¯ψ⟩E ∝
Z
τ=const.
(qµν

,¯xµ ¯ψ,¯xν + q0(¯x)¯ϕ∗¯ψ) dΣτ.
(2.80)
The functions qµν

structure of the wave equation. A common example of such an energy norm is derived from
the energy-momentum tensor of a scalar field, where the integrand components reflect the
field’s kinetic and potential energy contributions. The product (2.80) is positive-definite
and makes the non-self-adjoint character of the operator ˆL evident [310, 351]. Indeed,
calculating the formal adjoint with respect to the energy norm ⟨¯ϕ|ˆL ¯ψ⟩E = ⟨ˆL† ¯ϕ| ¯ψ⟩E
yields ˆL† = ˆL + ˆL∂

about the energy loss at the horizon and at future null infinity.
As an example, Fig. 12 shows the pseudospectra for the Schwarzschild spacetime
(Regge-Wheeler operator) constructed under the energy norm (2.80). Open contour lines
in the complex plane indicate spectral instability: small perturbations of the potential
completely destabilize the QNMs overtones. The astrophysical implications of this
phenomenon are discussed in Section 2.6 below.
Despite the solid mathematical foundation of this program, there are some
technical open issues.

Figure 12. Schwarzschild pseudospectra based on the energy norm. Open contour
lines extending across the complex plane signify QNM spectral instability.
Small
perturbations to the wave equation’s potential cause significant destabilization of QNM
overtones [310, 351]. The color code corresponds to log10 ςϵ(s) with the ϵ-pseudospectra
defined in Eq. (2.79) with contour lines of ϵ =constant. Figure adapted from [310].
energy norm does not always converge to a fixed, nonvanishing value with increasing
numerical resolution [354–358]. In such cases, the continuum limit indicates that the
pseudospectrum goes to zero in the entire complex half-plane Re(ω) < 0. This result
would imply that any point ω in this half-plane is an eigenvalue of the operator, and not
only the discrete values corresponding to the well-known QNMs. Thus, the numerical
calculation of pseudospectral contour lines under the energy norm offers a correct
qualitative understanding and interpretation of the underlying structure of the operator,
but quantitative assessments must be performed carefully [354–358].
The origin of this feature is not directly related to the particular numerical scheme
used in the calculation, but it is rooted in the definition of QNMs. The intuitive notion
that QNMs are mode solutions to Eq. (2.4) that “forbid waves to travel in from infinity
or out of the event horizon of the BH” (see Section 2.1) is a necessary, but not sufficient
condition [3, 359, 360]. In other words, outgoing boundary conditions at the horizon and
at infinity are not the only defining structure of QNMs, as there exist further unphysical
solutions satisfying such boundary conditions [3, 66, 305, 343–346] precisely in the entire
complex half-plane Re(ω) < 0. The numerical nonconvergence of the pseudospectrum in
the energy norm captures this subtlety.
The traditional definition of QNMs as poles of the Green’s function [3, 359, 360]
removes the ambiguities from the intuitive approach based on the boundary conditions.
By exploiting the hyperboloidal framework, an alternative definition for QNMs arises
as isolated eigenvalues of the infinitesimal generator of time translations acting on an
appropriately defined Hilbert space [66, 342–346, 361].
This definition relies on an inner product controlling higher derivatives of the
functions belonging to the functional space. Such product generalizes the energy norm,


=====PAGE 60=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
60
and it is often known in the mathematical literature as the Hp Sobolev norm
⟨¯ϕ| ¯ψ⟩Hp ∝
p
X
k=0
Z
τ=const.
qµν
k (¯x)∂k
¯µ ¯ϕ∗∂k
¯ν ¯ψ dΣτ.
(2.81)
Then, QNM eigenfunctions belong to the so-called Gevrey-2 class: roughly speaking, they
are more regular than a smooth function, but less than an analytic function [342–346, 361].
In this context, recent investigations show that the higher-order derivatives in the Hp
scalar product permit to address certain technical issues in the calculation of the
pseudospectrum, providing a better mathematical control of the convergence of the
pseudospectrum and offering additional insight into the stability properties of the
overtones [312, 362].
All in all, the discussion in this section summarizes the ongoing effort to tackle open
mathematical questions underlying the theory of BH QNMs. By revisiting seminal works
from the nineties [337, 338] with novel approaches and strategies, the scalar products
listed here have found application in the study of many physical problems, such as
mode avoidance (Section 2.3), environmental perturbations (Section 2.6), nonlinearities
(Sections 4.4 and 4.5) and tails (Section 5.3). There is an ongoing effort to explore and
synthesize these different strategies to establish a unified understanding of QNM theory
that reconciles their orthogonality properties, (in)completeness, non-self-adjointness, and
the geometric insights offered by the various methods.
2.6. Spectral instabilities and environmental effects
Initial contributors: Cardoso, Destounis, Duque, Panosso Macedo
So far, we have reviewed the structure of the QNM spectrum for electrovacuum
spacetimes. This assumption is justified for BHs in isolation, but the Universe is filled
with various forms of matter, including interstellar clouds, gas, plasma and dust in
galaxies, dark matter, populations of compact objects, and cosmic radiation. There
are hints that some of the GW events already observed by ground-based detectors
may originate in gas-rich environments, ranging from somewhat controversial claims
of electromagnetic counterparts to BBH mergers [363] to statistical hints that at least
a fraction of the observed BBH population may have originated in Active Galactic
Nuclei [364]. In fact, there is an ongoing effort to place direct bounds on the density of
the medium surrounding these BBH merger events [365]. The effect of environments is
definitely a concern for space-based instruments like LISA that will target the merger
of supermassive BH binaries in galactic centers, where the two BHs are brought to
coalesce by dynamical friction and migration driven either by gas or stars [366, 367].
It is therefore important to ask how astrophysical environments will affect the BH
spectroscopy program [368, 369]. This is the topic of this section.
One would expect that the BH response should be only slightly perturbed in the
presence of external matter with sufficiently small density, localized away from the BH
light ring. However, numerical investigations revealed the surprising fact that even small


=====PAGE 61=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
61
perturbations of the BH potential, due to e.g. astrophysical environments, can lead to
considerable changes in the BH vibrational spectra, potentially orders of magnitude away
from the QNM frequencies computed in vacuum [310, 337, 338, 352–354, 368, 370–375].
This phenomenon is known as spectral instability.
The BH spectroscopy program is based on perturbative calculations of the vacuum
BH spectrum in GR [376–378] and it implicitly assumes a reasonable physical robustness
against “small” changes in the system. Thus, understanding spectral instabilities is of
paramount importance for the interpretation of GW data.
2.6.1.
Spectral instabilities There are two major classes of spectral instabilities:
fundamental mode and high overtone instabilities. A simple toy model illustrating
fundamental mode instabilities in the context of massless perturbations of nonspinning
BHs (see Section 2.1) is helpful to better understand the issue. To mimic possible
environments, let us assume the master equation to be of the form of Eq. (2.7), but with
a perturbed potential Vϵ [368, 372]:
d2Ψ−
2
dr2
∗
+

(2.82)
where the tortoise coordinate is defined as dr/dr∗= 1 −2M/r, and a small, localized
“bump” is added to the effective potential V −
2 in vacuum (see Fig. 13), i.e.,
Vϵ ≡V −

(2.83)
Note that when ϵ = 0, Eq. (2.82) is equivalent to the RWZ equation (2.7), i.e., it governs
gravitational perturbations around nonrotating BHs in GR. The functional form of the
unperturbed potential is not crucial for the discussion, and for simplicity we will consider
the Regge-Wheeler potential V −
2 defined in Eq. (2.8).
In this ad hoc modification, external environments conspire to produce the “bump”
located at r∗= L, such that Vbump goes to zero as r∗→±∞. In Fig. 13 we consider two
possible choices for Vbump, i.e., a Pöschl-Teller [379] potential and a Gaussian:
Vbump = sech2 (r∗−L) ,

The QNM spectra of the perturbed potentials are shown in Fig. 14 (top left panel) for

under a perturbation of order ϵ, while ϖ is the actual perturbed dominant/fundamental
mode – i.e., the mode with the smallest (in absolute value) imaginary part, which
does not necessarily coincide with ω(ϵ). For small L, ϖ is initially quasi-stable and it
coincides with the perturbed fundamental mode of the original potential, ω(ϵ), which
spirals outward from its unperturbed Schwarzschild value (thick gray line). Notice how
the fundamental mode can change by a factor of two or more even for such a small value
of the perturbation amplitude ϵ, indicating spectral instability.

as the distance L between the bump and the BH (marked next to the purple dots) is
increased, for a Pöschl-Teller bump and ϵ = 10−6. Bottom left: Variation of the real

area designates the region of L where the new fundamental mode ϖ has been completely
destabilized with respect to the scale of the perturbation ϵ. Top right: “Phase diagram”
illustrating the regime where the three kinds of instabilities occur in the (ϵ, L) plane

As L increases, something even more dramatic happens: the trapped modes localized
between the two potential barriers migrate downwards and to the left in the complex


=====PAGE 63=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
63
plane. Eventually (at L ≃34) one of these modes overtakes ω(ϵ). This causes the first
of several discontinuous jumps in ϖ (shown with dotted lines, and highlighted in the
bottom-right panel). Figure 14 illustrates that, quite independently of the functional
form of Vbump, the fundamental mode ϖ is unstable in large regions of the relevant phase
space ([L, ϵ] for the Pöschl-Teller bump, and [L, σ] for the Gaussian bump). The nature
of this FD instability, its relation with TD waveforms, and the location of the edges
between the various regions were clarified analytically in various works [380, 381].
The crucial observation is that “two bump” systems have distinct scales. When
the characteristic distance L between the bump and the peak of the unperturbed BH
potential (located near the light ring) is much larger than the other scale in the problem
(i.e., the BH mass), a cavity is formed by the two potential peaks. This cavity supports
“trapped modes” of frequency ∼1/L which are trapped as long as 1/L2 ≪ϵVbump. In
these conditions, the fundamental BH QNM is destabilized [368, 372, 374, 382]: for
any arbitrarily small ϵVbump there is a scale L where an extremely low-frequency mode
appears ends up dominating over the fundamental BH QNM associated with the light
ring [8, 382, 383], and this low-frequency mode is never “close” to the original fundamental
mode (“close” meaning that the mode perturbation is at most of order ϵ [382]). Similar
phenomenology occurs if new scales are present close to the horizon, under the form of
matter or new boundary conditions [8, 383]; see e.g. [154, 357, 380, 381, 384] for more
detailed analyses of the spectra of double-peak potentials and of the consequent spectral
instabilities, and [357] for a discussion of the high energetic content of the apparent
“small” bump. The mode avoidance and resonance phenomena discussed in Section 2.3
occur quite generally in double-peak potentials [154].
The above discussion shows that the fundamental mode is generally unstable when
a second (large) length scale is present in the problem. What about the overtones?
In an attempt to capture possible ultraviolet quantum effects and to identify
spectral instabilities under small-scale changes to the effective potential, one can use
the notion of the ϵ-pseudospectrum defined in Section 2.5.4 to predict the occurrence
of spectral instabilities without the need of introducing ad hoc perturbations of order
O(ϵ) of the BH potential [310]. The pseudospectrum is very useful to understand the
phenomenon of QNM spectral instability in perturbation theory beyond the usual modal
analysis [310, 311]. Mathematical tools from the theory of non-normal operators [308, 309]
were successfully adapted to gravitational physics through a careful treatment of the
QNM boundary conditions, in which a geometrical strategy was used to adapt the time
coordinates to the asymptotic spacetime structure around the BH horizon and in the
wave zone, i.e., at future null infinity [303, 310] (see Section 2.5.4). An appropriate
norm is necessary to measure the “strength” or “smallness” of the perturbation: see
the discussion around Eq. (2.80).
The energy norm is well-defined under certain
conditions [310, 351, 357, 374], and it is a useful discriminator to understand why
the spectrum is destabilized under apparent “small” perturbations of the potential [357].
By plotting pseudospectral contour lines, that identify the maximal migration of
perturbed QNMs in the complex plane under a perturbation of order |ϵ| ≪1, one


=====PAGE 64=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
64
finds that asymptotically flat [310, 311, 385, 386], de Sitter [387–389], and anti-de
Sitter [355, 356, 390, 391] BHs all exhibit spectral instabilities.
See discussion in
Section 2.5.4 and fig. 12.
An example of spectral instability in the high-overtone regime concerns charged
BHs [5, 370, 371, 374, 392–395]. The asymptotic structure of the spectrum for any
massless field perturbation of nonspinning, neutral BHs is
Mω ∼log 3 −i(2n + 1)π
8π
+ O(n−1/2) ,
Q = 0 ,
(2.85)
in the large-n limit [5]. On the other hand, the asymptotic spectrum of slightly charged
BHs is found to be
Mω ∼log 5 −i(2n + 1)π
8π
+ O(n−1/2) ,
Q →0 ,
(2.86)
As a consequence, for any arbitrarily small charge Q the BH spectrum differs by O(Q0)
from the spectrum of a Schwarzschild BH. This is a clear example of spectral instability
in the large-n regime [374].
To conclude, a few important remarks are in order concerning the spectral instability
of both the fundamental mode and overtones. Despite the large shift to the spectrum
found above, in all realistic cases studied in the literature the corresponding early-time
signal in the TD is smooth and receives only small corrections (as quantified e.g. by
the unfaithfulness, see Section 2.6.2). In other words, the initial prompt response in
the TD is very close to the vacuum BH signal, while the destabilized modes dominate
only at late times. The information of the dominant ringdown at early time signals is
imprinted in the phase shift defined analogously to quantum scattering [396], which is
only perturbatively modified even with the spectral instability. This fact can also be
easily captured by causality argument for signals propagating in a cavity [368, 382]: at
late times, the double-bump-cavity filters the high-frequency content of the outgoing GW,
and all that remains is the new low-frequency fundamental mode, which is approached via
a sequence of “echoes” [8, 383, 397–400] (see Section 3.3 below). The second observation
concerns the existence of actual physical systems that produce “double-bump” potentials
as solutions to the Einstein field equations. There are no known viable astrophysical
setups whose perturbations to the gravitational field are described by a single decoupled
master equation as in Eq. (2.82). Indeed, there are indications that spacetimes described
by a double-peak effective potential have at least two light rings, and are therefore not a
small perturbation of a vacuum BH spacetime [374].
Spectral instability has nevertheless been observed and studied in a consistent
description of BH environments [401–403], which requires coupling matter to gravitational
perturbations. No matter how small the strength of the coupling, the gravitational
sector will always be “contaminated” (or destabilized) by the mode content in the matter
sector. However, there is now widespread consensus [368, 374, 382, 396] that the prompt
ringdown is only perturbatively affected, as discussed in detail in Section 2.6.2 below.


=====PAGE 65=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
65
2.6.2.
Environmental effects The analysis outlined above leads to the intriguing
conclusion that astrophysical environments can, in principle, produce considerable
changes in BH QNM spectra. What is left to understand is how the perturbed QNMs
appear in the TD ringdown signal, as computed from self-consistent solutions of the
Einstein field equations.
This question is of paramount importance because large
deviations from the vacuum predictions, if they occur in nature, may either hamper
our ability to infer the properties of BHs or (if properly understood) carry important
information on BH environments.
There are several physical mechanisms that can affect the BH spectroscopy program
in astrophysical environments (see [368] for a thorough list and simple order-of-magnitude
estimates). Here we will focus on the effect of galaxies, dark matter halos, and dark matter
spikes. To understand why environmental corrections could be relevant in this context,
recall for example that the BH at the center of the Milky Way, with a mass M ∼4×106M⊙,
is surrounded by stars and dark matter of total mass MH ∼5 × 1011M⊙within a radius
of aH ∼20 kpc (corresponding to aH ∼104 MH in geometrical units) [404, 405].
In the simple case of spherical symmetry, a fully relativistic solution can be found in
closed form [401]. Other matter distributions are, of course, possible, but the qualitative
conclusions discussed below are expected to hold also in other cases [406].
The qualitative setup is shown in Fig. 15. A BH of mass M is surrounded by a halo
of matter (stars, dark matter, etc.) of mass MH with characteristic length scale aH. The
environment’s compactness C and typical density ρ are given by
C = MH
aH
,
ρ ∼MH
a3
H
.
(2.87)
For the Milky Way, an order of magnitude estimate yields C ∼10−6 [405]. In general,
the halo size and mass are orders of magnitude larger than the BH’s.
Stationary and spherically symmetric solutions to the Einstein equations for an
anisotropic fluid surrounding a BH are known in closed form for specific density
profiles [401], but similar procedures can be used to model generic astrophysical
environments around BHs either analytically or numerically [406–416]. Given these fully
relativistic background spacetimes, computing geodesic motion and perturbations is a
relatively simple matter [401, 402, 406, 410, 415, 417].
The scales in the problem can be sufficiently distinct to make the computation
of QNMs in the general case challenging. However the axial sector of gravitational
perturbations does not couple to matter (under mild assumptions, such as being
nondissipative [335, 418]), and it is easier to handle [401, 419]. An accurate calculation
of the corresponding QNMs relies on casting the wave equation in terms of an eigenvalue
problem of a (non-self-adjoint) operator, achieved by means of the hyperboloidal approach
described in Section 2.5.4 [66, 304, 305].
The resulting (nonvacuum) axial QNM frequencies are shown in Fig. 16, and they
clearly depend on the properties of the BH environment. As the compactness C of
the halo increases from 10−6 to 10−1, the QNM frequencies ω20n are redshifted towards

Figure 18. Signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) required to distinguish the ringdown signal
from vacuum for different values of the halo compactness, C, and halo mass, MH. For a
chosen SNR (corresponding to some horizontal line) and halo mass, only values of C
to the right of the intersection between the horizontal line and the colored curves are
distinguishable from vacuum. The black horizontal line represents a putative signal
from Sgr A∗with mass ratio 10−5. The right axis shows the corresponding mismatch

curve. The shaded regions are estimates of extrapolation errors in the waveform. Figure
adapted from [403].
dominated by a “tail,” i.e., a superposition of a large number of power laws [420] due
to waves backscattering off the nonzero curvature of the background [155, 421, 422].
These terms slowly decay, leaving place to a single power law h+ ∼t−p (with p ∈N), as
discussed in Section 5.3 below. In the presence of a halo of matter, one observes instead
a slowly decaying fluid mode with characteristic period T ∼aH/cs,r, where cs,r is the
speed of sound in the radial direction, and with amplitude determined mostly by the
halo’s compactness. The excitation of this fluid mode is an interesting example of how
spectral instabilities can arise quite generically via mode coupling [374]. In conclusion,
astrophysical environments can significantly alter the QNM spectrum of a BH, but these
modifications in the spectrum do not necessarily imply that the signal will drastically
change at early times, when it has the highest amplitude.
A measure of the contribution of environmental effects in the ringdown signal is
the so-called “faithfulness” between the numerical signal generated in the nonvacuum
case and the numerical vacuum waveform [403]. In real observations the BH mass is a
priori unknown, so the faithfulness must be maximized over a “stretching” of the time
component which is different at each spacetime location, to model redshift as the wave
propagates. The result of this calculation is shown in Fig. 18. For example, for a halo
configuration with MH = M and aH = 58M, the faithfulness is maximized for a redshift


=====PAGE 69=====
2. The quasinormal mode spectrum in general relativity
69
factor 0.987, whereas 1 −MH/aH = 0.982. For smaller values of C, the mismatch follows
a power-law decay. This allows extrapolation to even smaller values of C, for which
numerical calculations are computationally prohibitive.
Are differences between the ringdown template and the real signal measurable at
very large signal-to-noise ratios? Current observations from the motion of the S2 star
in our own Galactic center limit the additional mass enclosed within its orbit to be
≲103 M⊙[423], but distributed over a semi-major axis aS2 ∼2 × 104MSgr A* (in units of
Sgr A* mass). Figure 18 suggests that for a typical galactic halo compactness C ≲10−4
the SNR must to be larger than several thousands to detect any environmental effects
other than the gravitational redshift. Therefore BH spectroscopy is not expected to be
able to probe astrophysical environments, even in the optimistic case of a signal from a
plunge of a 40 M⊙compact object into Sgr A* (unless the plunge happens to occur in
some exotic, highly compact environment).
Recent work explored NR simulations of collapsing and merging neutron stars
leading to BH formation, and used them to perform BH spectroscopy by extracting
QNMs as a function of the starting time [424]. The resulting ringdown signals can be
classified into “clean”, “modified”, and “distorted” cases, depending on the amount of
matter present in the system. Even in the presence of matter, possible deviations of the
QNM frequencies from the expected Kerr values seem to be dominated by ringdown
modeling systematics. Incorporating multiple QNMs drastically reduces mismatches and
errors in estimating the final BH mass at early times. If not treated carefully, deviations
in the fundamental QNM might artificially be overestimated and falsely attributed to
the presence of matter or violations of GR.
In summary, the last decade has seen substantial progress in our understanding
of how astrophysical environments affect BH spectroscopy. The emerging consensus is
that environments have a small impact, but further research is necessary to confirm
this finding. First of all, the conclusions summarized above are mostly based on the
assumption of a time-independent, nonrotating backgrounds. As discussed in Section 4.5.3
below, mass accretion can change the QNM spectrum [299, 335, 425–427], but in most
situations of astrophysical interest the resulting modifications are expected to be negligibly
small [368, 428]. The ringdown radiation produced by spinning BHs within astrophysical
environments is poorly understood. Tidal modifications to the spectrum of Schwarzschild
and Kerr BHs also deserve further scrutiny [429]. Even if environmental modifications
to the vacuum QNM spectrum turn out to be large, it may be possible to test the BH
nature of the underlying spacetime in other ways (e.g. through perturbative quantities
that may be observable and have been found to be largely unaffected by instabilities in
the spectrum, such as the greybody factors [396, 430, 431]).


=====PAGE 70=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
70
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
Ci sono soltanto due possibili conclusioni: se il risultato conferma l’ipotesi, allora hai
appena fatto una misura; se il risultato è contrario all’ipotesi, allora hai fatto una scoperta.
(There are only two possible conclusions: if the result confirms the hypothesis, then you
have just made a measurement; if the result disproves the hypothesis, then you have made
a discovery.)
Enrico Fermi
Part of the current experimental interest in BH spectroscopy comes from the
possibility of finding experimental smoking guns of new physics or modifications of
GR in the large and growing catalog of GW events. This possibility has motivated an
intense effort, reviewed in this chapter, to (i) compute QNM spectra in specific classes of
modified theories (Section 3.1); (ii) develop theory-agnostic ringdown parameterizations,
similar in spirit to the parameterized post-Newtonian formalism [432] (Section 3.2); and
(iii) investigate the effects of physics beyond the Standard Model that could produce
characteristic signatures – e.g., “echoes” – in the ringdown waveform (Section 3.3).
3.1. Theory-specific spectra
Initial contributors: Blázquez-Salcedo, Cano, Chung, Khoo, Yunes
In recent years, there has been substantial progress in understanding the QNM
spectrum of rotating BHs in theories beyond GR and the Standard Model of particle
physics. Most of the work concerns higher-derivative theories that modify the Einstein
equations in a continuous way in terms of free parameters (coupling constants). The
regime where these couplings are small and the new theory is close to GR is currently
the best understood scenario, but there are also results that apply to large couplings.
The space of possible theories is too large to enumerate [433, 434]. The theories we
will discuss here can be split into two main classes.
The first class assumes a pure metric theory, for which a general effective field theory
(EFT) extension of GR [435, 436], up to eight derivatives, reads

is a scalar constructed from the curvature tensor. The quantities V (Φ), Q, and f(Φ)
determine the nature of the gravity theory. For example, for the class of Einstein-scalar-
Gauss-Bonnet (EsGB) theories [437, 438], Q = G ≡R2 −4RαβRαβ + RαβγδRαβγδ, where

A theory with two scalars that couple simultaneously to the Gauss-Bonnet and
Pontryagin invariants can also be considered [441]. Such a theory is a natural prediction
of string theory [442], where one of the scalars is the dilaton and the second one is an
axion dual to the Kalb-Ramond two-form. However, QNM calculations in this theory
are in their infancy, hence it will not be discussed below.
For other classes of theories beyond the higher-derivative corrections discussed above,
the study of QNMs is less complete. One such example concern so-called DHOST theories,
for which it is not possible (in general) to write down the linearized perturbations in a
compact Schrödinger-like form [443–445]. Moreover, the presence of additional terms
and couplings may change the implementation of the boundary conditions [446]. A
possible way out for studying perturbations of BHs in alternative theories of gravity
is to use full-fledged TD numerical relativity (NR) evolutions. Although the required
numerical resolution to extract QNMs is not always met, a few preliminary results in


=====PAGE 72=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
72
this direction have been achieved [447–449]. One of the biggest challenges in studying
linear perturbations and computing QNMs in Lorentz-violating theories of gravity is that
BH solutions in these theories possess multiple horizons, and there is ongoing research
on how to impose boundary conditions [450]. There are exceptional cases (such as a
subclass of the so-called Hořava gravity theories [451]) in which all of the additional
degrees of freedom have the same propagation speed as the gravitational ones, but the
QNM spectrum in these cases is indistinguishable from GR.
The remainder of this section is structured as follows. We first review rotating BH
solutions in the theories defined by the actions (3.1), (3.4), which are no longer given by
the Kerr metric. Then we discuss several methods to analyze perturbations and QNMs
in these (and more general) theories. Finally, we summarize current state-of-the-art
calculations of QNM frequencies in specific cases.
3.1.1. Modified Kerr black holes
The calculation of the modified Kerr metric describing
rotating BHs in the theories under consideration is not a completely solved problem:
there are no exact analytic solutions for BHs with arbitrary rotation and coupling. Most
current efforts focus either on analytical approximations valid for small couplings or
slow-rotation, or on numerical solutions.
Small-spin and small-coupling expansion A common strategy to obtain corrections to
the Kerr metric is to find approximate analytical solution through a double series
expansion in small angular momentum and small coupling [452–458]. While the small
coupling approximation can be justified from the perspective of EFTs and current tests
of GR [432, 434, 459–461], restricting to small spin is not sufficient to describe the BH
remnants resulting from binary mergers, which typically have dimensionless spin χ ≃0.7.
This problem can be addressed by extending the spin expansion to very high orders [436].
The corrected Kerr metric is typically written in Boyer-Lindquist like coordinates
(t, r, x, ϕ), where x = cos θ, as

inefficient when approaching extremality (see e.g. [464] for a discussion in dCS gravity).
Numerical solutions Full numerical approaches are typically required to search for
solutions with arbitrary rotation and coupling constants, or solutions with nontrivial
coupling functions f(Φ). For example, in Einstein-dilaton-Gauss-Bonnet (EdGB) gravity
(a member of the EsGB class Q = G with V (Φ) = 0 and f(Φ) = e−γΦ, where γ is the
dilaton coupling constant), both static spherically symmetric [438, 465] and rotating
configuration [466–469] have been obtained. Static spherically symmetric [465, 470]
and rotating BH solutions [469, 471] are also known in linear EsGB gravity (another
member of the massless EsGB class, but with coupling function f(Φ) = Φ). Interestingly,
when the coupling function satisfies certain conditions (examples of which include
f(Φ) ∝Φ2 [472–475] and f(Φ) ∝exp(−Φ2) [476]), BHs can become “spontaneously
scalarized,” with a stable branch of modified BH solutions arising nonperturbatively
for large enough curvature: see [477] for a recent review. In dCS gravity, static and
spherically-symmetric BH solutions are described by the Schwarzschild metric just as in
GR, while rotating configurations were studied in [469, 478–481].
To obtain stationary and axially symmetric BHs one typically uses “quasi-isotropic”
spherical coordinates, with the generic metric ansatz
ds2 = −fdt2 + b
f
 dr2 + r2dθ2
+ l
f r2 sin2 θ(dϕ −w
r dt)2,
(3.9)
where f, b, l, w are functions of r and θ. With this ansatz for the metric, together with
the background field Φ (also a function of r and θ), the field equations are reduced
to a system of elliptic, PDEs that are solved numerically. The metric and scalar field
functions satisfy a number of boundary conditions that are derived from requiring the
standard conditions for astrophysical BHs: regularity of the solution on the rotation axis,
asymptotic flatness of the metric at spatial infinity, and regularity of the Killing horizon.
The domain of existence of the resulting solutions in some modified theories can
sometimes have interesting properties, as shown e.g. in Fig. 5 of [468] for EdGB gravity.
For instance, in EdGB gravity with γ = 1 and fixed α, the scalar charge of the solution


=====PAGE 74=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
74
grows as the mass of the static BH decreases, and eventually a critical solution with a
minimum mass is reached [437, 438, 482]. At this point, the BH mass increases again
along a small unstable branch of solutions before a singular solution is reached. For an
analysis of this branch structure with other values of γ, see [483]. The situation is similar
when introducing angular momentum [466–468]: for fixed values of coupling constant α
and χ, there is a BH solution with minimum mass. For large enough angular momentum
there is no second branch, and the singular solutions also possess a minimum mass. For
even larger values of the angular momentum, it is possible to reach a set of extremal
BHs in EdGB gravity. Hence, the boundary of the domain of BH existence is given by
the set of static EdGB BHs, the set of critical solutions, the set of extremal BHs, and
the set of Kerr BHs.
3.1.2.
Black hole perturbations in modified gravity: methods Besides the lack of
analytical BH solutions in modified gravity, other challenges plague the analysis of
perturbations in these theories. First of all, these solutions are not of Petrov type D [484],
and the Teukolsky equation is not valid. Separability is also generically lost, even in the
case of the wave operator for a scalar field. In addition, the equations of motion for these
theories are extremely intricate, containing higher-order derivatives and/or extra degrees
of freedom. For these reasons, up until recently, QNM frequencies were known only
for static and slowly rotating BHs [440, 458, 485–494], since these can be computed by
conventional methods using metric perturbations. These results are useful to learn about
some generic features of beyond-GR effects, like the breaking of isospectrality (we will
say more about this below), but they are not applicable to the typical angular momenta
of post-merger BHs. Two different classes of methods, reviewed below, have been devised
to overcome these challenges and compute QNMs in the case of large rotation.
Modified Teukolsky equation The first approach aims at extending the Teukolsky
equation [15] to encompass theories of gravity that permit algebraically general BH
spacetimes. It was first constructed within the Newman-Penrose formalism [306, 495], and
reformulated using the Geroch-Held-Penrose formalism [496]. Practical implementations
of this “modified Teukolsky formalism” have been developed for higher-derivative
gravity [496–498] and dCS gravity [499, 500]. The main idea behind these approaches
is to find an equation, valid for arbitrary theories, that reduces to the usual Teukolsky
equation when the theory reduces to GR and the background is of Petrov type D. Any
equation with such properties is enough to perform perturbation theory in a regime close
to GR.
The key is the introduction of a two-parameter expansion scheme, which helps
circumvent certain algebraic constraints due to the possible non-Petrov-type-D nature of
the background BH spacetime. Using ζ (denoting one of the dimensionless couplings
defined in (3.3), (3.5) above) to characterize the deviation from GR and ϵ to characterize
the strength of gravitational perturbations, one can expand all geometrical quantities in


=====PAGE 75=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
75
a bivariate series. For example, the Weyl scalars Ψn with n ∈(0, 4) can be expanded as
Ψn = Ψ(0,0)
n
+ ζΨ(1,0)
n
+ ϵΨ(0,1)
n
+ ζϵΨ(1,1)
n
+ · · · .
(3.10)
The quantities Ψ(0,0)
n
and Ψ(1,0)

represent
the Weyl scalars induced by GWs in GR and their leading-order, beyond-GR corrections,
respectively.
To generalize the Teukolsky equation one can consider the same combination of
Bianchi identities of the Riemann tensor that lead to the usual Teukolsky equation, but
without making any assumptions on the form of the curvature tensor, the equations
of motion, or the Petrov type of the background spacetime. Using these combinations
and the two-parameter expansion, the perturbed Newman-Penrose equations can be
decoupled to obtain the modified Teukolsky equation at first order in (ζ, ϵ)

They depend on the polarization of the perturbation, hence leading to a breaking of
isospectrality. For EsGB and dCS gravity, a similar process can be implemented by
decomposing the scalar fields in spheroidal harmonics. In this case, one expects that
scalar and gravitational modes of different ℓwill be coupled, leading to a set of coupled
radial PDEs that need to be solved numerically. In the case of dCS gravity, these coupled
Teukolsky radial equations have been analyzed at first order in the angular momentum
expansion [499], and the corresponding QNM frequencies have been computed [500].
Further insights can be gained by employing the parity operator [509]. This operator
enables separation of the modified Teukolsky equation into two distinct components:
an odd-parity equation coupled to the scalar field perturbations and an even-parity
equation that remains entirely decoupled, thereby exhibiting isospectrality breaking in
dCS gravity. Similarly, in EsGB gravity, one obtains an even-parity equation coupled to
the scalar field perturbations and an odd-parity equation that remains entirely decoupled,
again exhibiting isospectrality breaking.
All these findings are consistent with those obtained through the metric perturbation
framework discussed below, reinforcing the validity of both approaches [440, 483, 486,
489, 490, 493, 510–515].
The METRICS approach A different approach, known as Metric pErTuRbations
wIth speCtral methodS (METRICS) [516, 517], models both gravitational and scalar
perturbations by expressing them in a product decomposition (by peeling off a certain
asymptotic factor) and then applying a spectral expansion in Legendre polynomials
of (cosine of) the polar angle and Chebyshev polynomials of a (compactified) radial
variable [516, 517]. The linearized field equations then become a linear algebra problem
for the spectral coefficients and the QNMs frequencies.


=====PAGE 77=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
77
The METRICS approach is as follows. First, small perturbations of both the
background metric (g(0)
µν ) and scalar field background (Φ(0)) in Boyer-Lindquist-like
coordinates are written as
gµν = g(0)

Φ = Φ(0) + ϵ eimϕ−iωtˆhϑ ,
(3.19)
where ˆhµν and ˆhϑ are functions of r and χ only, and the quantity ϵ ≪1 is a perturbative
bookkeeping parameter. Enforcing the Regge-Wheeler gauge, the metric perturbation is
completely specified by 6 unknown metric variable hj=1,...,6 [13], plus ˆhϑ. The linearized
field equations governing hj=1,...,7 read
X

where β and γ are nonnegative integers, Ck,β,γ,j are complex functions of (r, cos θ) that
also depend on (M, a, m, ω, ζ), and k ∈[1, 11] labels the equations (10 metric field and 1
scalar field equation). Instead, the upper limit of β and γ depends on the theory. For
example, in GR and EsGB gravity, the summation ranges from β = γ = 0 to β + γ = 2;
in dCS gravity, 0 ≤β + γ ≤3. Assuming the expansion of Eq. (3.7), Ck,β,γ,j are rational
functions. Upon appropriate factorization and manipulation, Ck,β,γ,j can be converted
into polynomials [516, 517].
The divergent behavior of hj at the event horizon and spatial infinity due to
coordinate singularities, governed by the linearized field equations, is accounted for by
factorizing or “peeling off” an asymptotic factor Aj(r), which in GR reads
Aj(r) = eiωrr2iMω+ρ(j)

Here φp,l(r, cos θ) encodes all of the radial and polar angle dependence, which in the
METRICS approach is represented through a spectral expansion. That is, φp,l(r, cos θ)
is modeled through a complete and orthogonal spectral basis of r and cos θ (typically,
Legendre polynomials in cos θ and Chebyshev polynomials in a compactified radial
coordinate [512]); Nr and Nl are the spectral order of the spectral projection along the

78
radial and angular coordinates; finally, p and l label the degree of the spectral basis, with
vj,p,l constants. The approach has been validated in GR, showcasing its high accuracy,
with relative fractional errors smaller than 10−5 for all dimensionless spins below up to
0.95.
For EsGB and dCS gravity, this method simultaneously solves all 11 partial
differential equations for the 7 unknowns by transforming Eq. (3.22) into a generalized
eigenvalue problem. The solution has been found using the Newton-Raphson algorithm
and the generalized Moore-Penrose inverse [512].
Generalized spectral method (collocation) An alternative approach is the generalized
spectral collocation method [513]. The method can be used for a range of theories (e.g.,
linear EsGB gravity), but for concreteness is exemplified below for EdGB gravity. Under
linear perturbations of the metric tensor and dilaton fields, Eq. (3.19), the equations
take the form
Gµν = G(bg)
µν + ϵ δGµν(r, θ)ei(mϕ−ωt) = 0 ,
(3.23)
S
= S(bg) + ϵ δS(r, θ)ei(mϕ−ωt) = 0 .
(3.24)
where the subscript “(bg)” denotes background quantities (see [513] for the full
expressions). The background can be generic and numerical if desired. The perturbations
are also generic and do not require special coordinates, leading to results valid beyond
the small-coupling and small-spin approximations. Rotating background solutions satisfy
G(bg)
µν
= 0 and S(bg) = 0, leaving a system of coupled PDEs in r and θ for the perturbation
functions, δGµν = 0 and δS = 0. Imposing standard QNM boundary conditions and
regularity on the rotation axis yields a set of constraints through a perturbative analysis
of the asymptotic behavior at asymptotic infinity and near the horizon [513].
The perturbations are then expanded using Chebyshev polynomials and Legendre
functions, employing a compactified radial coordinate z = (r−r+)/(r+1) and x = cos(θ).
After the Chebyshev and Legendre expansions of the perturbation functions, the partial
differential system can be expressed as a quadratic eigenvalue problem
 M0 + M1ω + M2ω2⃗
C = 0 ,
(3.25)
with square matrices M0, M1 and M2 that depend on the background solution.
At variance with the METRICS approach, the quadratic eigenvalue is obtained
directly, without factorization and simplification of the perturbed field equations, on
a discretized grid of Nx × Ny points (typically of order 20 in both directions). The
unknown expansion coefficients that lie in the vector⃗C can be accurately determined.
One can then find accurate numerical solutions [513] for the eigenvalue ω, for example
through Maple and MATLAB with Multiprecision Computing Toolbox Advanpix [518].
The method has been validated in the GR rotating case, confirming its high accuracy: at
least 10−6 accuracy up to about 80% of the maximum angular momentum [519]. Besides
the modified gravity results covered below, this method was also applied to study the
QNMs of rapidly rotating wormholes [520].

see Eq. (3.26). Left panel: 2m0 modes for BHs of spin χ ∈[0, 0.85]. Right panel: 2m1
