3.1.3. Black hole perturbations in modified gravity: results
We now summarize the state
of the art of QNM frequency calculations that applied the methods described above to a
variety of theories.
EFT extensions of GR In these theories, defined by the action (3.1), the modified
Teukolsky equation allows one to obtain the modifications to the Kerr QNM frequencies
to linear order in the coupling constants ζq:
ωℓmn± = ωKerr
ℓmn + ζq
M δω(q)
ℓmn±(χ) + O
 ζ2
q

.
(3.26)
Here ± denotes the mode polarization, and we have introduced the factor of 1/M so
that the target of the computations, δω(q)
ℓmn±(χ), is dimensionless.
The frequency shifts can be found by solving Eq. (3.17) through, e.g., a generalized
continued fraction method through a higher-step recursive relation [521] or eigenvalue
perturbation techniques [208, 306]. The frequency shifts can also be obtained by mapping
Eq. (3.17) to the parameterized formalism reviewed in Section 3.2.3 below. Achieving a
reliable result for large angular momentum requires computing Eq. (3.17) at very high
order in the spin expansion, which is computationally challenging.
Shifts δω(q)
ℓmn±(χ) for all the EFT theories, for the modes ℓ= 2, 3, 4, m ∈[−ℓ, ℓ],
n = 0, 1, 2 and up to angular momentum χ ∼0.7 −0.9 were computed up to expansion
order χ18 [498]. In Fig. 19 we show the (ℓ, m, n) = (2, m, 0), (2, m, 1) modes in the
even-parity cubic theory case. The results agree with previous computations for static
and slowly rotating BHs obtained via modified RWZ equations [458, 487, 492] and are
publicly available [522] (see Appendix A).
One of the most generic beyond-GR effects is the breaking of isospectrality, so


=====PAGE 80=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
80
that even and odd modes receive different corrections [523, 524]. This works differently
for parity-preserving and parity-breaking theories. In the former case, δω+ and δω−
are unrelated to each other, while in the latter δω+ = −δω−. Interestingly, combining
parity-preserving corrections (with shifts δωpres±) and parity-breaking ones (with shifts
δω± = ±δωbreak) the total shift is not just the sum of each of them. Instead, the following
combination rule holds [492, 497, 524]
δωtot± = 1
2 (δωpres+ + δωpres−) ±
r
1
4 (δωpres+ −δωpres−)2 + δω2
break .
(3.27)
This relationship arises from the coupling between perturbations of different parity.
While all of the analyzed higher-derivative theories break isospectrality, it has been
recently observed that the special quartic theory C2 + ˜C2 preserves isospectrality in the
eikonal limit [274]. In this theory, isospectrality is still broken for low-ℓmodes, but the
difference between even and odd modes is small. Additional results for other theories
and more modes are available online [522] (see Appendix A).
These results have recently been applied to test EFT corrections against real
ringdown data from LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA (LVK) observations in [525], finding a bound
on the scale of new physics ℓ≲35 km. Future ground-based detectors will substantially
improve these bounds, as discussed in Section 7.2.2 below.
dCS gravity In this theory, the QNM frequencies are also obtained as a linear expansion
in the coupling, similar to Eq. (3.26). Results are known for the static and spherically
symmetric (Schwarzschild) background [485, 510, 524, 526], and for rotating (non-Kerr)
BHs at linear order in the small-spin expansion [440, 490, 499] and for χ up to ∼0.75
using METRICS [527]. All of these studies relied on a metric perturbation approach,
except for [499], which employed the modified Teukolsky approach, expanded to linear
order in the spin.
All the existing calculations show that, in dCS gravity, the axial frequency is
shifted more significantly than the polar frequency, due to the stronger coupling of the
perturbations of the pseudoscalar to axial metric perturbations. In particular, when
the background has zero spin (a = 0), the perturbations of the pseudoscalar field
completely decouple from the polar metric perturbations. Fig. 20 shows the lead-order
QNM-frequency shift in ζ (i.e. δω defined by Eq. (3.26)) of the axial metric perturbations
for the ℓmn = 220, 330 and 320 modes of a rotating BH background with 0 ≤χ ≤0.753.
Observe that δω of the polar perturbations to nonrotating BHs is zero for all modes,
because the polar metric perturbations are decoupled from the dCS terms when χ = 0.
Analytical fitting expressions of the QNM-frequency shift obtained by METRICS that
are valid for χ up to 0.75 are available in [527], and obtained by a separate analytical
calculation valid up to linear order in the spin are also available [490].
Linear EsGB gravity Initial results for this theory were obtained using either numerical
solutions or analytic (slow-rotation) approximations for the background metric and
scalar field [483, 486, 489, 493, 528]. The extension to rapidly rotating BH backgrounds,

ζEsGB is defined in (3.5). The figures confirms the breaking of isospectrality, as in the
case of cubic EFTs and dCS gravity. Fitting formulas and calculations for other modes
are also available [512].
For small spin, the numerical results are consistent with second-order in spin
perturbative predictions [483, 486, 489, 493], confirming that polar frequency corrections
are shifted by a larger extent compared to axial ones. The relative fractional uncertainty
of the real (imaginary) frequency varies between [10−8, 10−2] ([10−6, 10−1]), depending on
the spin [511]. This level of accuracy is good enough to analyze individual ringdown GW
signals observed by the LVK ground-based detectors in the third observing run [460, 529].
The polar frequencies were used to construct an effective one body (EOB) waveform
model [530] employed to search for modified gravity signatures in GW data (see
Section 6.3.2).
A calculation of the QNM frequencies in linear EsGB gravity that is fully
nonperturbative in the coupling can be found in [514].
EdGB gravity In Fig. 22 we show fully nonperturbative results for the QNM frequencies
of EdGB BHs for a dilaton coupling with γ = 1 as a function of coupling constant ζEdGB
for two representative values of the spin. The frequencies of the longest-lived modes
with ℓ= 2, 3 and m = 2 were computed using the spectral collocation method described
above [513], and the vertical lines mark the critical values of ζEdGB beyond which no
regular BHs exist in EdGB theory for the given dimensionless spin χ = J/M2.
As we see in Figure 22, the isospectrality of the Kerr axial-led and polar-led modes
is broken in EdGB gravity. This happens already in the static case [483, 486], becoming
more prominent as the coupling strength and BH spin increase, approaching the boundary

of the domain of existence of BH solutions. The figure also shows the scalar-led modes

field on the Kerr background as the coupling strength is decreased. The lowest value
of the frequency MωR always corresponds to the ℓ= 2 metric modes. Typically, polar
modes have smaller values of MωR, but the difference with respect to the axial modes is
always small.
Another interesting property of the spectrum is the switch of mode dominance as the
critical coupling strength is approached, and the scalar charge of the solutions increases.
While for Kerr the gravity-led modes are always longer lived than the scalar-led modes,
here, depending on the angular momentum and coupling strength, it is possible to find
solutions for which (vice versa) the scalar-led modes are longer lived than the gravity-led
modes. This dominance switch is displayed in Fig. 22 for χ = 0.4 and ℓ= 2, stressing
the importance of scalar-led modes when modeling the ringdown phase in EdGB theory.
These general results also reproduce with excellent accuracy the static limit [483, 486]
obtained with a different shooting method, and the analytical predictions in the slow-
rotation approximation [489, 493]. The computed QNMs satisfy all the PDEs and
boundary conditions of the perturbations with an accuracy of at least 10−4. More details
can be found in [513] as well as [515], where the modes with a negative real part are also

Future
directions Despite
the
remarkable
progress
summarized
above,
several
outstanding problems remain in order to complete our understanding of the linear
ringdown in theories beyond GR. An important open question is the case of very highly
rotating BHs, χ ≳0.95. None of the methods reviewed above has achieved reliable
results in this regime yet, as this is technically challenging and may require alternative
approaches [531] (see also Section 2.1.5). The computation of overtones has also been
quite limited so far. In addition, some of the results we have discussed only consider
corrections to the QNMs perturbative in the coupling. For the purpose of testing these
theories, it is important to have reliable predictions for the QNM frequencies for arbitrary
values of the coupling constants. Beyond the QNM frequencies, one should also analyze
the effect of beyond-GR physics on the QNM amplitudes [494] and, more broadly, on


=====PAGE 84=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
84
the TD signal. In a context where extensions of GR are treated as EFTs, it is important
to have reliable predictions for the QNMs for arbitrary values of the coupling constants
or an estimate of the regime validity of QNM calculations. This validity regime may
depend on the harmonic or overtone number [494, 498, 532].
Addressing these issues is important in order to perform accurate ringdown-based
tests of theories beyond GR with next-generation (XG) GW observatories (see Section 7).
3.2. Theory-agnostic parameterization
Initial contributors: Franchini, Kimura, Völkel
Rather than computing QNM frequencies on a theory-by-theory basis, an alternative
approach is to develop a theory-agnostic, parametric framework to compute QNMs when
the underlying theory of gravity or the corresponding BH solutions are unknown. This
approach is strongly motivated by the large amount of modified theories of gravity or new
physics scenarios that could induce deviations from the Kerr spectrum in the data. Note
however that the terminology “theory-agnostic” can be somewhat misleading, as specific
theoretical constraints are often imposed, either implicitly or explicitly. For example,
if we start from the ansatz that modifications to the QNM spectrum can be described
by modifications of the perturbation potentials in GR we must necessarily assume
separability, which selects only certain classes of beyond-GR theories (see e.g. [533]).
Similarly, the eikonal limit (see Section 2.4), may not hold in some theories; approaches
using parameterized BH metrics requires a choice of test fields (e.g., the Klein-Gordon
equation in a background spacetime); and so on.
Any parameterization must resolve a tension between generality and simplicity. The
parameterization should be general enough to encompass the largest possible amount of
new physics, yet limit the number of free parameters so that they will not be degenerate
or unconstrained, thus making measurements uninformative. If the aim is to understand
fundamental properties of QNMs beyond GR without focusing on a given theory, one
must assume some general structure for the equations of motion or even for the action of
the theory, either starting from first principles or using known cases as a guide. Finally,
assuming a metric theory, one might be interested in linking the modifications to the
QNM spectrum (as induced, e.g., by environmental effects) to modifications in the
underlying spacetime metric.
Despite these limitations, theory-agnostic approaches allow us to study a rich
phenomenology capturing several classes of modifications, and they provide a valuable,
interpretable observational framework to search for new physics.
In this chapter
we review the most commonly used parameterized models. We first introduce the
simplest parameterization of possible deviations from the gravitational QNM spectrum in
Section 3.2.1, then we discuss the “parameterized spectroscopy” (or ParSpec) framework
in Section 3.2.2, and finally we discuss parameterizations of the perturbation potentials
in Section 3.2.3. In Section 3.2.4 we describe approaches based on parameterized BH
metrics and ideas based on the eikonal limit (see Section 2.4). Finally, in Section 3.2.5


=====PAGE 85=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
85
we discuss parameterizations at the level of the action and the so-called “EFT of QNMs.”
3.2.1. Agnostic QNM deviations
Quite generally, if there are any deviations from
vacuum GR, the gravitational QNMs can be parameterized in the form
fℓmn = f Kerr
ℓmn (1 + δfℓmn) ,
(3.28)
τℓmn = τ Kerr
ℓmn (1 + δτℓmn) ,
(3.29)
where here f refers to the real component of the complex QNM frequency ω, and τ to
the inverse of its imaginary component. This parameterization has been proposed
and used extensively in GW data analysis for tests of GR and searches of new
physics [460, 529, 534–536] (see also Section 6 below). The parameterization is extremely
flexible and general, as it can accommodate any shift to a given QNM with respect to
the Kerr spectrum. Its main limitation is that Eq. (3.28) does not take into account
new modes that are not given by continuous deformations of the Kerr gravitational
spectrum which may appear in the signal (see [537, 538]). In general, additional modes
and polarizations will appear in the spectrum whenever a specific modified theory of
gravity involves additional degrees of freedom (such as dynamical scalar, vector or tensor
fields). While the parameterization will not capture the power contained in such new
modes, any additional degrees of freedom should only contain a small fraction of the
signal power if the modified theory is close enough to GR. In this sense, the deviation
parameters are still expected to capture the main modifications to the GR signal. Any
deviation from the Kerr spectrum would flag the detection of an anomalous feature, that
can later be followed up with informed searches including additional modes.
From the data analysis point of view, the main disadvantage of this parameterized
model is that it requires two new degrees of freedom for each mode (ℓ, m, n) to describe
potential deviations from the GR spectrum. For this reason the parameterization can be
very inefficient when the number of observed modes increases, resulting in uninformative
measurements (see Section 6). A second important limitation is that the parameterization
is “too general,” in the sense that it does not fold in the dependence of the frequencies and
of their modifications on the BH parameters (mass and spin). This makes it impossible
to exploit the properties of the underlying source population: individual sources with
different masses and spins are better at probing different modifications from GR, and by
combining multiple sources it should be possible to obtain better bounds on any specific
modification. More fundamentally, if a deviation is detected, one would like a physical
understanding of its origin – something which is not provided by Eq. (3.28).
In conclusion, the parameterization of Eq. (3.28) is useful for null tests, but it cannot
be used to extract the underlying physics without further assumptions. Due to the
generality of the parameterization, it is also very difficult to give a physical interpretation
of nonzero deviation parameters in multi-modal scenarios if the underlying GR signal
model is incomplete. This can happen when the number of detectable modes is unknown
(and especially for overtones), even in idealized situations [378, 539–541].


=====PAGE 86=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
86
In the following we will show how including more assumptions based on the
underlying physics affects QNM parameterizations.
3.2.2.
The ParSpec framework A simple and reasonable ansatz is to assume the
deviations from GR to be small, i.e.,
δfℓmn ≪1 ,
δτℓmn ≪1 .
(3.30)
This ansatz is based on the reasoning that even if modifications of GR are present in
nature, the stringent experimental bounds imposed by existing tests of GR [432, 434,
460, 461, 529, 542–546] imply that the modifications must be hidden in the noise, and
the relative size of any frequency modifications to the Kerr spectrum can be expected to
be much smaller than unity. From a theoretical perspective, this assumption fits well
with an EFT description of gravity, where GR is the low-energy limit of some as yet
unknown theory (see Section 3.1 for theory-specific examples).
A phenomenological model for “universal” modifications of spinning BH spectra that
incorporate the key features of the remnant mass and spin dependence was proposed
in [547]. In this parameterized spectroscopy, or “ParSpec” model, the modifications are
written in the form of parameterized ringdown spin expansion coefficients as follows:

Depending on the underlying theory of modified gravity, we can consider three
different cases:
(i) Scale-free corrections If the corrections to GR are given by a single dimensionless

ℓmn. This case includes
certain scalar-tensor and Lorentz-violating theories, such as Einstein-Aether and Hořava

(ii) Single dimensionful coupling The corrections to GR are given by a dimensionful

captures the scaling of the curvature-dependent modifications in EsGB and dCS gravity,
and for p = 6 it captures the scaling of some of the EFTs discussed in Section 3.1.
(iii) Individual charges BHs in the modified theory possess what is sometimes called
“primary hair,” i.e., a new charge Q that is completely independent on the BH mass

and spin, with dimensions [Q] = ˆ

(see [549] for a review of “hairy” BH solutions). In this case, we can set γ = Q/Mp
s .
This parameterization was used to derive constraints from current observational
data in the case of scale-free and dimensionful couplings [550], in the case of individual
charges with p = 2 (i.e., Kerr-Newman BHs) [551, 552], and in some specific theories
of gravity with selected values of p [553]. Projected bounds with XG detectors were
computed in [554] (see Sections 6.3 and 7.2 for an extensive discussion).
3.2.3. Parameterized QNM framework
This section deals with the parameterized QNM
framework, which is one step closer to the theory-specific case. Instead of assuming
a mass and spin dependence in the parameterization of the QNM spectrum, in this
framework one assumes a parameterization of the effective potentials. In this way we
can make direct contact with the numerous specific models in which the perturbation
equations can be cast in the form of a modified RWZ equation (in the nonrotating case)
or of a modified Teukolsky equation (in the rotating case). The power of the method
is that one can compute the QNM spectrum in all of these beyond-GR theories in one
sweep without assuming any specific theory. The main (important) assumption is that,
in the perturbative spirit discussed above, the modifications of the RWZ or Teukolsky
potentials must be small, in a sense that can be made precise. We will first focus on the
nonrotating case for simplicity, and then generalize the treatment to rotating BHs.
The parameterized QNM framework was first introduced to compute linear
QNM corrections due to small modifications of single-field (scalar, gravitational axial,
gravitational polar) perturbation equations of the Schwarzschild BH [523]. Then it was
extended to include quadratic corrections, systems with multiple coupled fields [524],
QNM overtones [532, 555] and the TD evolution of initial data [556].
In the nonrotating or slowly rotating case, let us assume that single or multi-field
perturbations Φ are well approximated by the wave equation

another illustration of the spectral QNM instability discussed in Section 2.6: overtones
are easily destabilized by near-horizon modifications of the effective potential [532, 565].

3.2.4. Parameterized black hole metrics with test fields and the eikonal approximation
In the following, we discuss two closely related approaches for exploring QNMs beyond
GR. We start by studying test fields on a parameterized BH background, and then we
outline how the eikonal limit can be used in this context.
While the parameterized QNM framework from Section 3.2.3 connects QNM
deviations with modifications of the effective potential, it cannot directly be used to infer
the background metric unless the equations of motions beyond GR are being provided
(e.g., in a theory-specific application). However, deviations from the GR QNMs in terms
of the background spacetime deviations can be studied under certain assumptions – for
instance considering test-field perturbations (as discussed below), or assuming the validity
of the eikonal limit (discussed in Section 2.4). Since perturbations of test fields, e.g.
the massless Klein-Gordon equation on the background spacetime, share qualitatively
similar properties with gravitational perturbations in GR, one might still identify certain
smoking gun features of new physics. A comprehensive overview of the literature on
parameterized BH metrics is beyond the scope of this review [567–574]; here we discuss
QNM studies based on some of the most commonly used parameterizations.
The simplest cases are those where the background metric is static and spherically
symmetric. Here, the master equation for a massless scalar field is simply given by

such that these static, spherically symmetric parameterized metrics can be “reverse
engineered” to be exact solutions of the theory, so that one can consistently compute the
QNMs in the full theory [577]. The QNM overtones are very sensitive to the properties
of the parameterized metrics close to the horizon [565].
The problem becomes significantly more complicated for rotating BHs because even
test fields do not, in general, yield separable perturbation equations. Some progress is
possible for special classes of axisymmetric BHs for which separability holds [578]. In
particular, scalar QNMs of rotating spacetimes that describe deviations from Kerr BHs
through a single parameter have been analyzed using Leaver’s method [579]. There has
also been some progress on computing the QNMs of nonseparable scalar perturbations
in non-Kerr backgrounds, with analytical or numerical tools [507, 508, 580].
Next we outline how the eikonal approximation, reviewed in Section 2.4, has been
used in the present context. The key aspect of the “eikonal limit” (the limit of large
angular numbers ℓ) is that the QNMs of perturbations induced by test fields can be
directly related to properties of the unstable photon orbit at the light ring.
This
connection implies that, in GR, the QNM frequencies can be expressed solely in terms of
the properties of the background metric. This is a very convenient simplification that
opens a variety of theory-agnostic tests based on beyond-Kerr spacetimes.
The correspondence was first established by connecting the QNM frequency to the
peak of the effective potential and to circular photon orbits [228, 581]. It has then been
shown that the orbital period Ωand Lyapunov exponent λ can be used to approximate
both the real and imaginary part of the fundamental QNM [67, 68, 234, 582]. This is
also in agreement with a WKB analysis [68, 237], yielding
ω = Ωℓ−i

(3.41)
Further GR-related studies can be found in [46, 47, 68–70, 583].
In a series of works [68, 236, 491, 584–586], the eikonal limit was also studied
in theories beyond GR. In a theory-agnostic approach, one could attempt to use
the eikonal limit in modified theories of gravity in order to compute QNMs from
the modified background metric, provided there are no additional fields that lead to
coupled perturbation equations [584]. However, the correspondence does not always
hold [587, 588], in particular when considering theories with additional scalar or vector
field that lead to coupled perturbation equations. Moreover, a practical limitation of
the method is that small-ℓmultipoles are dominant in GW data, whereas the eikonal


=====PAGE 91=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
91
approximation (while still accurate at the percent level even for ℓ= 2) is more accurate
for large ℓ. Despite complications due to its low accuracy for the dominant quadrupolar
mode ℓ= 2, the eikonal approximation has been applied to obtain bounds for different
types of modifications to the Kerr spacetime using both current (observational) and
future (simulated) GW data [589–592].
3.2.5. Effective field theory of QNMs
Yet another possibility for parameterizing the
QNMs of a gravity theory is to do so directly at the level of the action.
In the
Schwarzschild case this can be done by listing all the possible covariant terms at first
order in perturbations, including possible contributions from scalar and vector fields [445],
following a covariant version of the formalism developed for cosmological perturbations
in [593, 594]. A more general result is the EFT of QNMs of any spherically symmetric
metric plus a scalar field coupled to gravity [595]. By assuming a spin-2, unitary, Lorentz
invariant theory of gravity coupled to a spin-0 field, perturbations of a generic nonrotating
background metric of the form

3.3. New physics at the horizon
Initial contributors: Afshordi, Maggio, Pani


=====PAGE 92=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
92
3.3.1. Motivation
Einstein’s theory of GR is in agreement with all of our experimental
evidence for BHs so far, but it leaves unsolved some of the deepest theoretical puzzles
unveiled by these mysterious objects. In particular, the semi-classical picture of Hawking’s
BH evaporation is incompatible with quantum unitarity, the microstates underlying the
enormous BH entropy are unknown, and the singularities hidden by the BH horizon
signal a breakdown of classical physics inside BHs. These far-reaching problems are
dramatic manifestations of the incompleteness of our fundamental laws of nature.
Motivated by Penrose’s weak cosmic censorship conjecture [597], the incompleteness
of GR has traditionally been overlooked in observations of astrophysical BHs, as it was
assumed to manifest only near singularities, safely concealed behind event horizons.
However, a drastic violation of the classical causal structure of event horizons, beyond
small corrections [598, 599], appears necessary to evaporate away BH information. In
particular, the so-called “firewall paradox” [600] has been invoked to suggest that it is
impossible to reconcile the three (apparently modest) assumptions of locality, causality,
and unitarity outside regular event horizons if the entropy SBH of an evaporating BH
follows its horizon area AH = 4π(r2
+ + a2). The relation between the two, according to
Bekenstein and Hawking, reads:
SBH = AH
4Gℏ.
(3.45)
While the paradox only manifests itself about halfway through the BH evaporation (after
the so-called Page time tPage ∼G2M 3/ℏ), it has been argued [600] that due to the fast
scrambling of quantum states within BHs [601], a significant quantum modification of
the classical horizon, or “firewall,” would happen much earlier, i.e., within a “scrambling
time”:
tscr ≃
ℏ
2πkBTH
ln SBH = κ−1
+ ln
 AH
4ℏG

∼GM ln
GM 2
ℏ

≪tPage,
(3.46)
where TH = 2(r+ −M)/AH is the horizon (or Hawking) temperature, and κ+ is the
surface gravity of the event horizon. This coincides with the time scale to reflect a signal
off a mirror, within a Planck proper distance from the BH horizon [602].
An alternative derivation comes from studying a BH in equilibrium with a thermal
bath of radiation at the Hawking temperature. Due to the blueshift effect near the BH
horizon, the total entropy of the radiation bath (even within a finite box) is infinite.
However, if we consider the microstates of radiation near the horizon as contributing to
the BH entropy, then we would require Srad < SBH, which suggests that the free radiation
needs to be cut off by a “firewall” near the horizon. The inequality Srad < SBH provides
an upper limit on the time for null signals to be reflected off the firewall, which coincides
with the scrambling time in Eq. (3.46) [603]. If the bath temperature deviates from the
Hawking temperature, then the dynamical backreaction of the quantum vacuum stress
on the geometry would truncate the classical spacetime even farther out, resulting in
shorter time scales, as small as half of Eq. (3.46) [604].
The executive summary of the state of affairs is that the assertion of a Bekenstein-
Hawking area law for thermodynamic entropy (3.45) suggests significant deviations


=====PAGE 93=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
93
from classical GR dynamics near BH event horizons (e.g., [598, 605]). This challenges
the classical BH paradigm, and thus cosmic censorship, which posits that the fate
of the gravitational collapse of sufficiently massive and compact stars is a classical
BH, surrounded by regular event horizons. Therefore, horizonless compact objects can
emerge due to quantum modifications at the horizon scale (e.g., gravastars [606] and
wormholes [607–609]) or due to a modified theory of gravity describing the interior of
compact objects (e.g., fuzzballs [610–613] and nonlocal stars [614, 615]).
3.3.2. General signatures of horizon-scale structures
If the remnant of a compact binary
coalescence is a horizonless compact object, the QNM spectrum is predicted to deviate
from the BH one [383, 397], also showing a breaking of isospectrality between axial and
polar modes [399, 616–618]. When the horizonless remnant is ultracompact (i.e., featuring
a photon sphere [8]), a characteristic cavity in the effective potential of the perturbation
exists between the light ring and the object. In the TD, the prompt ringdown signal
would be nearly indistinguishable from the signal from a BH, since it is excited at the
light ring. After a travel time in the cavity of the effective potential, an additional signal
could be emitted in the form of GW echoes [383, 397, 619]. Therefore, GW echoes in
the postmerger phase of a compact binary coalescence are possible smoking guns for the
formation of a horizonless ultracompact object [2, 397, 620, 621] (see also [622–625]).
Assuming that these horizonless compact objects are stabilized outside a proper Planck
length of the would-be classical horizon results in echo time delays that precisely match
the quantum scrambling time τecho = tscr of Eq. (3.46) in generic circumstances [626].
Since their conception, GW echoes have been studied in a variety of contexts,
including near-horizon quantum structures [383, 397, 627], ultracompact stars [621, 628–
631], BHs in modified theories of gravity in which the graviton reflects effectively on
a hard wall [632, 633], or any other scenarios producing a BH effective potential with
multiple peaks. Echoes have also been studied in the fuzzball scenario. The cavity
reflections of low-energy scalars can be expressed as a sum over the number of bounces at
the throat of these geometries [634, 635]. More recently, GW echoes have been studied
for specific BH (or fuzzball) microstates in string theory [636–638].
The cavity picture of GW echoes we have presented so far is semi-classical, and
limited to linearized perturbations. Therefore, it is fair to ask whether such a naive
description would survive a fully quantum and/or nonlinear treatment, a point that we
shall come back to later in this subsection. However, it is interesting to note that a fully
quantum language for the very same dynamics was already proposed by Bekenstein back
in 1972 [639], in terms of quantized BH horizon area. Roughly speaking, the QNMs of
the cavity (significantly different and much more long-lived than those of a GR BH) have
frequencies ωn ≃2πnτ −1
echo + const., where n is an integer. If we conceive the ringdown
of a horizonless compact object as the spontaneous emission of gravitons, the gravitons
appear to be emitted at discrete energy quanta of ∆E = 2πℏτ −1
echo. Using the first law of
BH thermodynamics, κ+∆AH/(8π) = ∆E (and ignoring the BH spin for now), the BH


=====PAGE 94=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
94
area/entropy can only vary by a discrete amount:
∆AH = 16π2ℏG
κ+τecho
.
(3.47)
This suggests that the discretization of the horizon area proposed by Bekenstein [639] is
directly related to the presence of echoes in the semi-classical cavity description [640].
This connection was also extended to spinning BHs [641], where the picture is more
complex [642]. The connection was initially proposed to apply to the prompt ringdown
QNM emission [643], but this would require a more drastic departure from current
understanding [644].
A similar quantum description of the emission mechanism for GW echoes can be
provided. Considering Hawking radiation as quantum spontaneous emission of gravitons
in vacuum, incident GWs onto a quantum BH should lead to quantum stimulated
emission [645], amplifying the amplitude of Hawking radiation. This stimulation is
caused by the classical infalling GWs during ringdown, which in turn can lead to the
emergence of GW echoes [627, 646].
Given the uncertainty on the nature of quantum physics that replaces the classical
description of event horizons, it is reasonable to seek a model-agnostic EFT approach
allowing for generic deviations, as a systematic expansion [647]. An ambiguity in this
approach is to decide on the correct small parameter (e.g., distance, frequency, energy,
curvature, or something else) that controls the expansion. For example, strictly local
EFT approaches to modifications of GR (e.g., [458, 648]) are unlikely to accommodate
the nonlocal or highly unusual physics necessary to replace horizons by ultracompact
objects, or to address the BH information paradox.
3.3.3. QNM spectrum beyond Kerr
If the remnant is not compact enough to feature
a photon sphere, cavity effects are absent, and so are echoes in the post-merger phase.
Nevertheless, the ringdown would still show deviations from the ordinary BH QNMs,
different QNM excitation [649], or excitation of new nongravitational modes [537] (e.g.
extra fluid modes in objects containing matter [544, 650], or scalar excitations in boson
star models [651–654]). These deviations are mostly degenerate with those predicted
by beyond-GR theories (see Sections 3.1 and 3.2). They may also resemble the post-
merger phenomenology of neutron star remnants, featuring long-lived modes, fluid-mode
excitations, and in general being qualitatively different from the ordinary BH ringdown.
The QNMs of exotic compact objects have been computed for boson stars [655, 656],
gravastars [657–659], BH microstates within the fuzzball paradigm [636–638, 660], and
generic compact objects described within the membrane paradigm [399, 618].
3.3.4. Echo morphology
For a classical BH, the ringdown phase can be understood
as the spacetime’s response to perturbations occurring near the photon sphere [661].
Part of the signal produced near the light ring propagates outward, reaching distant
observers, while the remainder travels inward towards the horizon, without influencing


=====PAGE 95=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
95
the observables at large distances. This occurs because classical BHs are by definition
perfect absorbers, with their horizon serving as a one-way, null hypersurface.
However, this property relies on the existence of a classical horizon and is dramatically
different for horizonless ultracompact objects or in any model predicting new physics at
the horizon scale (see [8] for a detailed overview). In this case, perturbations can probe
the inner boundary of compact objects (such as the regular core of an ultracompact
object, or a modified horizon) and reach an observer at large distance, carrying crucial
information about putative effects occurring at the horizon scale.
Simply by causality, the effects of the horizon-scale structure cannot show up in the
signal before a time of the order of the (round-trip) light crossing time from the photon
ring to the boundary. Assuming that the background spacetime is sufficiently close to
the Kerr metric, this time scale is no shorter than [383, 397, 662]
τecho ∼2M

1 + (1 −χ2)−1/2
| ln ϵ| ,
(3.48)
where ϵ ∼d2/M 2 ≪1, and d is the proper distance between the would-be horizon and
any putative effective surface. If such distance is Planckian, d ∼ℏ1/2 ≈10−35m, then
ϵ ∼S−1
BH, and this time scale is comparable to the scrambling time (3.46). Depending
on the model, the radiation can take significant time to probe the object’s interior,
in which case the time scale above should be augmented by the corresponding time
delay. Therefore, if the spacetime geometry is similar to Kerr, the prompt ringdown is
essentially indistinguishable from that of an ordinary BH for times shorter than τecho.
On longer time scales, the reflected pulse is semi-trapped between the object and
the light ring. With each interaction at the light ring, a portion escapes to distant
observers, producing a series of echoes with progressively diminishing amplitudes. These
reflections occur at intervals defined by a characteristic echo delay time, which is no
shorter than τecho. Remarkably, owing to the logarithmic dependence of Eq. (3.48), even
if the proper distance of the effective surface is Planckian, the delay time is only a factor
| ln ϵ| = | ln(ℏ/M 2)| (≈175 for a solar-mass object) longer than the BH light crossing
time 4M, and therefore potentially observable in the post-merger phase following the
prompt ringdown.
Consideration of possible exotic classical interiors for some ultracompact horizonless
objects have led to proposals for much longer (geometric) echo time delays [663–665].
However, as noted above, the quantum vacuum backreaction [604] appears to truncate
these geometries, and/or their entropy is likely to significantly exceed the Bekenstein-
Hawking entropy (3.45) [603]. This suggests that, even if such long echo time delays
with τecho ≳tscr exist, they cannot be understood within a semi-classical geometric
description.
The photon sphere barrier acts as a frequency dependent high-pass filter. The
characteristic frequencies governing the prompt ringdown are very similar to the BH
QNM frequencies, being essentially governed by the excitation of the photon sphere [383].
The frequencies governing each subsequent GW echo become progressively smaller and,

at very late times, the GW signal is dominated by low-frequency QNMs associated with
the partially reflecting boundary conditions at the inner boundary [398, 666, 667].
Echoes appear in the transient regime following the universal prompt ringdown,
and developing while the remnant reaches stationarity on long time scales. While such
echoes are generic smoking guns of nonconventional horizon-scale physics, their actual
detectability crucially depends on the object’s effective reflectivity, R, since the amplitude
of each subsequent echo is suppressed by a factor of R relative to the previous one (see
Fig. 24 for an illustration). The reflectivity is a model-dependent, complex function of
the frequency which can be conveniently defined from the asymptotic behavior of the
perturbations near the effective surface [399]
Ψ ∼e−iω(x−x0) + R(ω)eiω(x−x0) ,
(3.49)
where Ψ is a suitably-defined wave function describing the radial component of the
perturbation [669, 670], and x0 is the location of the effective surface in the tortoise
coordinate. The ordinary boundary condition for a classical BH is R = 0 for any frequency
(perfect absorption). On the contrary, an object whose interaction with GWs is negligible
would have |R|2 = 1 (perfect “reflection,” which can actually correspond to radiation
crossing the object without being absorbed). Overall, the frequency-dependent phase
factor in R accounts for any time delay within the object. As discussed in the next section,

parameters representing the echoes emission [8].

3.3.6. Nonlinear effects and echoes in numerical relativity
In closing, let us address the
question of whether our understanding of GW echoes (or any signature of new horizon
physics) may survive beyond linear perturbation theory. Energy loss due to emitted
echoes inevitably changes the background spacetime at third order in perturbation theory,
decreasing τecho for subsequent echoes [688, 689]. More drastically, one may imagine
that the expansion of an apparent horizon due to the backreaction of an infalling wave
packet may trap it before any potential reflection is possible [690] (but see [689] for a
counterpoint).
Arguably, the holy grail for modeling any nonlinear novel horizon physics in the
ringdown phase is its implementation into NR codes, which currently provide state-
of-the-art full gravitational waveforms from compact binary mergers. This is a hard
problem, especially given the uncertain nature of the quantum gravity physics under
consideration. Studies of the post-merger signal from coalescing boson stars in the large
mass-ratio regime [654] showcase the subsequent emission of bursts at high frequencies,
sourced by perturbations of the remnant’s internal degrees of freedom. However, in this
case the maximum compactness is far from the values discussed above, which makes a
systematic separation of ringdown and echoes impossible. Another approach [675] stitches
a numerical GR simulated volume into an outer region that obeys linearized perturbations,
with a dissipative dispersion relation that models Boltzmann echoes [627, 646]. Recently,
a numerical implementation of novel near-horizon physics has been carried out in the form
of a numerical solver for the GR initial-boundary value problem [689, 691]. While these
studies have provided examples of fully nonlinear evolution of GR within an effective
numerical “membrane paradigm,” the correct scheme to evolve this membrane depends
on the new physics responsible for its formation and thus remains a challenging open
problem, both theoretically and numerically.
3.3.7. Other proposals for new physics at the horizon scale
In addition to predicting
deviations in the standard BH QNMs, new modes in the ringdown, or echoes in the
post-merger phase, specific models can introduce other subtle effects.
For example, while models of horizonless ultracompact objects are expected to have
the same ringdown phenomenology at least qualitatively, peculiar effects departing from
this picture might arise. The ringdown of BH microstates emerging in the fuzzball
scenario is much richer and more complex than in other models due to the absence
of spatial isometries [636]. This introduces mode mixing and effectively quenches the
echoes, even in the absence of effective absorption.
In the frozen-star model [692, 693], the pressure is maximally negative in the radial
direction and vanishes in the tangential directions. The interior is not described by


=====PAGE 100=====
3. Ringdown beyond general relativity and the Standard Model
100
a classical geometry but, at the microscopic level, is described by a fluid of highly
excited, interacting, closed, fundamental strings.
Due to the peculiar equation of
state, perturbations in the interior vanish identically at the classical level, but display
low-frequency, long-lived modes whose lifetime depends on the string length, which is
parametrically longer than the Planck length [694].
Finally, if massless modes propagate at different speeds (as predicted, for instance,
in Lorentz-violating theories), each would possess its own distinct horizon. One mode
could potentially probe the interior of the other’s horizon, leading to energy extraction
from the BH and a distinctive late-time relaxation behavior [450].
Compact objects with an ergoregion but without a horizon might be affected by an
ergoregion instability when spinning sufficiently fast [186, 597]. The instability time scale
is much longer than the decay time of the BH ringdown and the GW echo delay time [669].
The ergoregion instability was studied in uniform-density stars [695], gravastars [696],
and boson stars [697]. The only way to prevent the instability is by absorbing the
negative-energy states in the ergoregion with dissipation mechanisms [669].


=====PAGE 101=====
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
101
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
One thought spectra are marvelous, but it is not possible to make progress there. Just as
if you have the wings of a butterfly, then certainly it is very regular with the colors and
so on, but nobody thought one could get the basis of biology from the coloring of the wing
of a butterfly. So that was the way to look at it.
Niels Bohr, Interview by T.S. Kuhn, L. Rosenfeld, E. Rudinger, and A. Petersen,
October 31, 1962, AHQP.
After decades of work, we now have a deep understanding of the ornate structure of
BH QNM spectra in GR and beyond GR. However, to make progress in the experimental
implementation of BH spectroscopy we need a similar understanding of QNM excitation;
more specifically, we must be able to predict the amplitudes of the dominant modes
resulting from a binary merger, and their dynamical excitation in the full nonlinear
theory. The mode amplitudes are usually computed by solving the radial perturbation
equation using Green’s functions techniques (Section 4.1), and then computing the
so-called “excitation factors” and “excitation coefficients” (Section 4.2). These basic
techniques have recently been extended to compute the QNM amplitudes at quadratic
order, both in Schwarzschild (Section 4.3) and in Kerr (Section 4.4). The analytical
results obtained in this way are complementary to more empirical approaches, where the
amplitudes of linear and nonlinear QNMs are extracted by direct fits of TD evolutions
of the perturbation equations for single perturbed BHs, or from NR simulations of BBH
mergers. These efforts are reviewed in Section 4.5.
4.1. Green’s functions
Initial contributor: Lagos
Standard spectroscopic tests of gravity are concerned with testing the exponential
temporal evolution of the signal by measuring the QNM frequencies of different angular
harmonics. However, the complete ringdown solution is characterized by additional
nontrivial temporal dependence, as well as a spatial evolution. Both of these bring
information about the causal structure of the signal, when QNMs are expected to
dominate the emission, and the process setting the initial conditions of ringdown
relaxation (e.g., the merger of a BBH system).
At linear order in perturbation theory, the ringdown signal satisfies the RWZ
equations (2.7) for Schwarzschild BHs and the Teukolsky equation (2.5) for Kerr BHs.
These are linear differential equations that can be formally solved with the Green’s
function approach.
The properties of the Green’s function of ringdown signals have been thoroughly
analyzed [155, 165, 241, 338, 359, 698–703]. Since the equations of interest have a
separable angular dependence in the FD, let us consider the following representative


=====PAGE 102=====
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes

Avoiding the exact ansatz in Eq. (2.3) is important for the solution to include not only

The exact form of the effective potential V2(ω, r∗) depends on the type of BH and
perturbation variable. For Schwarzschild BHs, V2(ω, r∗) matches the effective potentials
V ±
2 defined in Eqs. (2.8)-(2.9), which are purely real, and do not depend on m nor ω. For
Kerr BHs, the original radial Teukolsky equation (2.5) in terms of the Boyer-Lindquist
coordinates does not take the form of Eq. (4.1). Nonetheless, a change of variables for the
field will bring Eq. (2.5) into the form of Eq. (4.1) for the tortoise coordinate [704–706].
In this case, the potential V2(ω, r∗) of Kerr will generally depend on m and ω, and it
can be made real when ω is real [704, 706]. For both types of BHs, Schwarzschild and
Kerr, the effective potentials are short range. Specifically, the Kerr potential decays to
zero towards spatial infinity, and to V2 →ω2 −(ω −am/(2Mr+))2 towards the horizon
r+ [706]. For Schwarzschild, both potentials decay to zero in both limits.
A different radial field redefinition transforms the radial Teukolsky equation into
the Sasaki-Nakamura equation [18], which has the form of Eq. (4.1) with an additional
first-order derivative on the left-hand side, and a short-ranged potential that reduces
to the Regge-Wheeler potential when a = 0. The Green’s function for Kerr can also be
studied in the Sasaki-Nakamura formalism, but here our starting point will be that fields
are chosen such that no first derivatives are present, and hence Eq. (4.1) holds.

For a general value of ω, the boundary conditions for gout and gin cannot be satisfied
at the same time; therefore these are two independent solutions of the homogeneous
equation, and W̸ = 0. However, for ω values that coincide with the linear QNM spectrum,
gout and gin are given by the same single solution. In that case, Ain = Aout = 0 and thus
W = 0: see Eq. (4.11). As a consequence of the fact that G ∝1/W, Gωℓm has poles at
the (linear) QNM frequencies ω = ωℓmn.
The integral on the displaced real axis involved in the computation of the TD
Green’s function Gℓm, Eq. (4.1), is obtained by deforming the integration contour in the
complex-frequency plane according to the features of Gωℓm [155]. The main features
are: (i) it contains first-order poles at the linear QNM frequencies ω = ωℓmn [706]; (ii)
it contains a pole at ω = 0, due not to the Wronskian alone, but its combination with


=====PAGE 105=====
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
105
ℜ(ω)
ℑ(ω)
Figure 25.
Contour integration in the complex frequency plane for the Green’s
function. Black dots schematically show the simple poles corresponding to the linear
QNM frequencies with negative imaginary frequency. The contour contains a piece
above the real axis (blue short-dashed line), a large semi-circle (green long-dashed line)
and a piece surrounding the branch cut and pole at ω = 0 (red dotted line).
gout and gin; (iii) it contains a branch cut running along the negative imaginary ω values,
connected to the pole at ω = 0. According to these features, the Green’s function in the
TD may be obtained using the residue theorem, following a closed contour integral as
shown in Fig. 25.
As a result of the frequency integration, the TD Green’s function can be expressed
in terms of three contributions:
Gℓm(t, r∗|¯t, ¯r∗) = GQ + GF + GB,
(4.16)
with GQ, GF and GB known as the QNM, flat, and branch cut contributions, respectively.
(1) GQ comes from a sum over the residues at the linear QNM frequency poles (black
dots in Fig. 25), and leads to exponentials with frequencies ω = ωℓmn, thus producing
the usual QNM solutions. This implies that the linear QNM frequencies will be
contained in the ringdown signal, unless the initial conditions are fine-tuned [709].
(2) GF comes from the arcs of the semi-infinite circle of the integration contour (green
long-dashed lines in Fig. 25), and its contribution includes information about high-
magnitude frequency contributions and asymptotically far signals, that propagate
effectively in free space since they are insensitive to the potential. For this reason,
GF is expected to be responsible for the prompt response in ringdown solutions,
which will mostly depend on the details of the initial conditions.
(3) GB comes from the branch cut contribution (red dotted line in Fig. 25) and is
interpreted to arise from the long-range decay of the potential, corresponding
to low-frequencies. It generally leads to polynomial tails in ringdown solutions,
which can dominate the late-time signal, and are understood as the result of waves
back-scattering off the potential [710] (see Section 5.3).

4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
106

In the general case, the boundaries of these regions are expected to be fuzzy, but this
figure schematically illustrates the role of causality constraints. Horizontal blue lines
indicate the maximum size of the spatial region causally connected to (t, r∗) through
GQ and GF . Figure taken from [702].
While their complete expressions are not known, details on different asymptotic
limits for GQ and GB can be found in [155]. An intuition on their effect can be gained
by looking at the limit of large |r∗| and |¯r∗|, using the asymptotic forms of gin and gout
in Eqs. (4.7)-(4.8), leading to

where Θ(x) is the step function (unity if x > 0, zero otherwise), and W ′

The term fℓmn is a constant factor depending on the QNM frequencies ωℓmn, with
fℓmn = 1 when r∗and ¯r∗have opposite signs, fℓmn = Bin(ωℓmn) when r∗, ¯r∗> 0, and
fℓmn = Bout(ωℓmn) when r∗, ¯r∗< 0. The step functions in the above expressions represent
nontrivial causality constraints coming from how the contour in the ω integral is closed.
The causality constraints that ringdown solutions have to satisfy in the asymptotic
limit considered here are shown in Fig. 26. In this figure (¯t, ¯r∗) is interpreted as an event
with a nonzero initial condition, and (t, r∗) corresponds to the observer. In particular, the
step function for GQ implies that the QNM piece of the Green’s function does not vanish
only if the point (¯t, ¯r∗) is causally connected to (t, r∗) via the potential (approximated to
be at r∗= 0 in these asymptotic expressions). This means that QNM solutions will build
in time, as a given initial condition gets causally connected to the potential and then has
enough time to travel towards the observer. For initial conditions with compact support,
the QNM solutions will eventually reach a stationary regime, when the entire initial
condition has been connected to the potential and the observer, and the solution will


=====PAGE 107=====
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
107
Figure 27. Solution to the Regge-Wheeler equation with ℓ= 2 and spin 1, as a
function of time, for an observer at r = 25 (in units 2M = 1). The initial condition
is a Gaussian at r = 28 traveling toward the horizon. The first peak is the prompt
response, and the following oscillations correspond to the QNM solutions. Figure taken
from [155].
be well approximated by a superposition of QNM frequencies with constant amplitudes.
This causality constraint will thus lead to a time variation of the linear QNM amplitudes
beyond the complex exponential dependence in the initial ringdown regime (e.g., near
the merger of two BHs) [165, 378, 702]. The time taken to stabilize will depend on
the initial condition, as exemplified by calculations with toy models [709]. Since these
results were obtained in the asymptotic limit of the Green’s function, the actual causality
conditions at smaller radii are expected to be analogous but with fuzzier boundaries.
In addition, the causality constraints from GF imply that part of the initial conditions
will travel directly towards the observer. Compared to GQ, a signal produced by GF is
thus expected to arrive earlier than those produced by GQ. For this reason, GF is said
to generate a “prompt response.” The effect of GQ and GF has been studied with toy
model initial conditions [155, 165]. An example is given in Fig. 27.
The GB component does not appear in the asymptotic study of the Green’s function,
since the potential vanishes in that limit. However, a number of studies of its polynomial
form have been performed in other limits [155, 700, 710–714]. For instance, at late times
such that r∗, ¯r∗≪(t −¯t) and for static initial conditions one finds
GB ∼(r∗¯r∗)ℓ+1(t −¯t)−2ℓ−3 ,
(4.19)
which describes the propagation of low-frequency waves. From this expression, the
dominant multipole will have ℓ= 2, with higher multipoles decaying faster. This Green’s
function component also leads to polynomial tails in ringdown solutions. Since QNM
solutions decay exponentially fast, polynomial tails dominate ringdown signals at late
times. For a detailed discussion of their physical interpretation and impact on signal
modeling, see Section 5.3.
Analogous features for the Green’s function are expected for Kerr BHs. In particular,
as previously mentioned, the Schwarzschild and Kerr cases can both be cast in the form


=====PAGE 108=====
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
108
of Eq. (4.1), with the same asymptotic behavior towards the horizon and infinity. The
main difference between the two cases lies in the fact that the angular separation in
Kerr uses spheroidal harmonics, which depend on ω and may thus introduce additional
nontrivial time dependence on the TD Green’s function. As discussed in [700], which
analyzes the Kerr Green’s function to obtain late-time tail solutions, the ω dependence
of the spheroidal harmonics leads to apparent branch points that are not present for
Schwarzschild BHs, yet such angular branch cuts are spurious artifacts of the spheroidal
decomposition, which will vanish when performing the infinite sum over ℓto obtain the
full Green’s function. Analytical series approximations of the Kerr Green’s function have
been obtained in [156, 157, 715, 716] (see also the review [158]). The Green’s function
for a scalar field around a Kerr BH has also been studied in [699]. As opposed to the
Teukolsky formalism discussed here, a different approach to the Kerr Green’s function
useful for reconstructing the full metric perturbation hµν can be found in [703].
An important observation is that the Green’s function approach can also be used
to analyze the behavior of higher-order BH perturbations, iteratively. In fact, at any
perturbative order, perturbations will satisfy the same linear differential equation, with
the only difference being the presence of an additional source term fixed by lower order
perturbations. For example, second-order ringdown solutions Ψ(2)
2
satisfy the equation

∂2
r∗+ ω2 −V2(ω, r∗)

Ψ(2)
2 (ω, r∗) = S(2)[Ψ2],
(4.20)
where S(2) is a second-order source, a function of quadratic terms in the linear perturbation
Ψ2 and its derivatives. While a homogeneous solution to this equation will simply
renormalize the linear solution, its particular solution can be obtained with the Green’s
function in a way analogous to Eq. (4.6):
Ψ(2)

Z

(4.21)
Applications of the Green’s function approach to second-order ringdown perturbations for
Schwarzschild BHs predict the presence of nonlinear polynomial tails in toy models [717]
and shed light on the causal formation and evolution of nonlinearities [702]. Analogous
results are expected for Kerr BHs.
4.2. Kerr amplitudes in the time domain and excitation factors
Initial contributors: Oshita
4.2.1. Kerr perturbations
Gravitational waveforms in the Kerr background can be
computed by solving the Teukolsky equation (see Section 2.1). A useful feature of the
Teukolsky equation is that it can be separated into two ODEs for the angular and radial
part of the perturbations, Eqs. (2.4) and (2.5). The full waveform can be obtained by
solving the Teukolsky equation using the Green’s function approach, possibly including
a matter source term. In this “Teukolsky formalism,” an apparent obstacle is that


=====PAGE 109=====
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
109
the required integrals can be divergent. An appropriate regularization, equivalent to
introducing a Green’s function that is well-behaved at infinity, can resolve this issue [718].
Alternatively, the effective potential in the wave equation can be made short-range by
transforming the Teukolsky equation using the “generalized Darboux” or “Chandrasekhar”
transformation [705, 706, 719, 720], which reads
Xℓmω ≡α(r)Rℓmω + β(r)
∆
dRℓmω
dr
,
(4.22)
in terms of certain functions α(r) and β(r). By properly choosing the functions α
and β in (4.22), the radial Teukolsky equation is transformed into another ODE for
Xℓmω with a short-range potential (see e.g. [721] for a detailed review of the generalized
Darboux transformation). With a specific choice of α(r) and β(r), the radial perturbation
equation has a short-range real potential, and it is known as the Chandrasekhar-Detweiler
equation [705]. This equation features a potential with four distinct branches. For certain
combinations of the Kerr parameter and of the mode frequency, one of the potential
branches may become singular, necessitating a switch between branches. A different
choice of α(r) and β(r) leads to a second radial perturbation equation with a short-range
complex potential, known as the Sasaki-Nakamura equation [18], which (for perturbations
with s = −2) has the form
 d2
dr2
∗
−F(r) d
dr∗
−U(r)

Xℓmω(r) = Iℓmω(r).
(4.23)
The explicit expressions of F(r), U(r) and Iℓmω(r) are given in [18]. The Sasaki-Nakamura
equation is convenient for numerical work due to its short-range complex potential, and
it has also been extended to other values of the spin weight [722]. The equation reduces
to the Regge-Wheeler equation in the limit a →0. In the Sasaki-Nakamura formalism,
the source term Iℓmω is also short-range, hence the full waveform can be obtained by
performing a convergent integral. The calculation of the source term requires a double
integral of the Teukolsky source term, with two corresponding integration constants
determined so that the inhomogeneous solution of the Teukolsky equation is purely
outgoing at infinity [16, 18]. The source term of the Sasaki-Nakamura equation for a
point particle in geodesic motion on the Kerr background was computed in [18, 723, 724].
An important property of the source term is that it redshifts to zero as the particle
approaches the Kerr event horizon. Since solutions of the source-free Teukolsky equation
are QNMs, this ensures that the waveform for a plunging particle terminates in QNMs
which are phase-coherent with the inspiral preceding the plunge.
Another possibility to study the excitation of Kerr amplitudes is to directly solve
the Teukolsky equation (2.2) with a point-particle source, without switching to an
equation which uses a short-range potential [725, 726]. Key to this method is that the
source is approximated as a variant of an impulse response function which preserves
integral identities of the Dirac delta function and its derivatives. By combining adiabatic
backreaction data (see e.g. [727] for an overview) with a prescription to describe how


=====PAGE 110=====
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
110
the adiabatic evolution ends as the particle begins plunging into the Kerr BH, one can
construct a worldline describing a small body’s inspiral and plunge for a wide range of
initial conditions. This method, initially developed in [728], was extended to a larger class
of inspirals and improved in [729]. Given the worldline, the Teukolsky equation source
term (e.g. [341, 730]) and the gravitational waveforms can be computed by numerically
solving the evolution equation. The final cycles of these waveforms consist of QNMs,
so these time evolutions can be used to numerically study the relative excitation of the
modes as a function of the geometry of the point-particle orbit.

The Kerr Green’s function contribution to the TD waveform for the dominant
quadrupolar mode, hG,22(t −x), is shown in Fig. 28, and it exhibits a ringdown phase at
late times. The waveform is computed by numerically integrating the Sasaki-Nakamura
equation and by directly reading off Ain/out from the homogeneous solution. The TD
waveform is found by using a contour on the real-ω axis and an integration interval in
the range [−4M, 4M]. The full waveform ξ(t −x) shown in Fig. 29 was found by taking
the convolution with the numerically computed source term of [723], and it corresponds
to a particle plunging along an equatorial geodesic into a Kerr BH with varying spin,
neglecting radiation reaction on the particle trajectory.

using different methods to construct the source term.

complete gravitational waveforms in the extreme mass-ratio limit within the EOB

source term [37]. In other works, the loss of energy and angular momentum of the
small body was taken into account by generalizing the Ori-Thorne procedure [728] to
describe the transition from inspiral to plunge. For example, the dependence of QNM
excitation on the inspiral-to-plunge transition in the Schwarzschild background can
be studied using an analytical form of the trajectory [736, 737]. Several works have
explored trajectories describing the transition from inspiral to plunge [738–740] and the
worldline of small bodies following initially circular but misaligned orbits [729] in the
Kerr background. This work was the basis for subsequent studies of the excitation of
Kerr QNMs [741, 742] where the Teukolsky equation was solved with high precision in
the TD, following methods developed in [341, 730].
As in this approach it is not possible to split the QNM, prompt and tail components
of the Green’s function, the QNM amplitudes need to be extracted a posteriori from the
full solution by fitting a superposition of damped sinusoids with frequencies corresponding
to the QNM spectrum. This methodology is described in detail in Section 5.
Rather than finding the QNM amplitudes from fits of the gravitational waveforms,
an alternative is to analytically compute the excitation factor Eℓmn and the source
factor Tℓmn from first principles. The source factor calculation in the Schwarzschild case
was pioneered by Leaver [155]. It involves the integration of the QNM eigenfunctions
ξq(ωn, x) in Eq. (4.34) in standard Schwarzschild coordinates, which yields divergent
results at both the horizon and infinity. The source factor integral can be regularized,
either by subtracting divergent terms (as in [743]) or by analytical continuation of the
radial coordinate into the complex plane and contour deformation (as in [155], see
also [95, 494, 744, 745]). A similar source factor calculation for a particle plunging along
the spin axis of a Kerr BH, based on the regularization scheme developed in [743], can be
found in [164] (see also recent work in [746, 747]). A generalization of these calculations
to generic orbits is required to understand QNM excitation and to improve waveform
models for extreme mass ratio binaries, but it is technically complicated. A promising


=====PAGE 114=====

114
Figure 30. The absolute value of the excitation factor, |E22n|, for overtones up to
n = 20. The red marker indicates the peak of |E22n|. Figure adapted from [162].
avenue in this direction is the extension of self-force techniques [748] beyond the inspiral
phase [749, 750].
4.2.3. Kerr excitation factors
The excitation factors Bℓmn, defined as the residues of
the Green’s function at the QNM poles, quantify the “intrinsic excitability” of QNMs.
It is sometimes useful to introduce also the quantity Eℓmn ≡Bℓmn/ω2
ℓmn [162], which is
useful to take into account the fact that the strain amplitude h and the Newman-Penrose
scalar are related by two time derivatives (Ψ4 ∼¨h). The excitation factors were first
computed in the Schwarzschild case [155, 163, 165, 731, 744]. The Kerr case was initially
investigated in [163, 745], and later extended to n = 3 and up to ℓ= 7 [164], to n = 20
for ℓ= 2 [162], and to multipoles with ℓ> 2 [751]. The excitation factors are now known
in the scalar, electromagnetic and gravitational case up to ℓ= 7 and to n = 7, thanks
to technical improvements to achieve high accuracy [94, 95, 154]. Excitation factor
calculations require the homogeneous solution of the perturbation equation for general
complex frequencies, which can be achieved using the Mano-Suzuki-Takasugi (MST)
method [157]. This technique was applied in [154, 164]. In [95], the Sasaki-Nakamura
equation with a general complex frequency was solved instead. An alternative is to use
Heun functions to obtain the relevant expressions for the Kerr-de Sitter spacetime, and
then extrapolate to the Kerr case by setting the cosmological constant to zero [162, 752].
The high-ℓexpansion method was also applied to the computation of Schwarzschild
excitation factors [241, 707]. The accuracy of this method for low ℓ’s can be improved
by including higher-order terms [753].
The Kerr excitation factors are shown in Fig. 30 for ℓ= m = 2 and varying overtone
number. The absolute value of the excitation factor peaks around n = 5, 6. However,
the overtone excitation coefficients are ultimately weighted by the source factor, so it


=====PAGE 115=====
4. Quasinormal mode amplitudes
115
Figure 31. Absolute values of the excitation factors |Eℓmn| for (ℓ, m) = (2, 2), (3, 3),
(4, 4), and (5, 5), and for a = 0.8M. Figure adapted from [751].
Figure 32. Spin dependence of the absolute value of the excitation factors, |E22n|, for
overtones up to n = 10. Figure adapted from [162].
is not possible to draw definitive implications on the excitations of overtones in actual
ringdown signals just by looking at the excitation factors. The peak of the excitation
factor shifts towards higher overtone numbers for higher multipoles (see Fig. 31). As
shown in Fig. 32, E22n grows with spin for all overtones up to a ≃0.9M, at which point
the n = 5, 6 overtones display a peak, with the n = 5 overtone being strongly suppressed
in the near-extremal case. This behavior is caused by mode repulsion (see Section 2.3).


=====PAGE 116=====

ambiguous up to a factor of eiωℓmnt∗, where the parameter t∗encodes the ambiguity in
the starting time of ringdown, and it should be defined at a time such that the QNM
expansion is convergent [162, 751, 754], as carefully investigated in [754]. The ringdown
phase in hG,ℓm can be reconstructed using a superposition of QNMs weighted by the
excitation factors, hE:

4.3. Quadratic modes in Schwarzschild
Initial contributors: Bucciotti, Kuntz, Riotto
Until recently, most spectroscopy efforts have been dedicated to understanding
the physical excitation mechanisms and detectability of the linear ringdown spectrum.
However, a debate on the domain of validity of linear perturbation theory in describing
BH binary dynamics [161, 378] has sparked a community-wide initiative to systematically
investigate nonlinear effects, such as quadratic mode excitation, absorption-induced
changes in BH mass and spin, and transient effects. Among these, significant attention has
been given to the new modes appearing in the spectrum at higher orders in perturbation
theory [755–760]. The quadratic modes or quadratic QNMs (QQNMs) appearing at
second order are the topic of this section. These additional families of QNM frequencies
naturally arise from the nonlinear nature of Einstein’s equations, and were recently
confidently extracted from nonlinear binary merger simulations [378, 540, 761–763].
Following these breakthroughs, various analytical and numerical techniques were
swiftly employed to characterize the amplitudes and the dependence on initial data
of these modes for Schwarzschild and Kerr BHs [277, 279, 297, 297, 764–772]. These
predictions are currently being incorporated as additional components in the construction
of BBH waveform templates. Remarkably, not only the QQNM frequencies are calculable
from perturbation theory, but also their relative amplitudes with respect to the linear
modes can be fully obtained within perturbation theory only. This makes quadratic
modes very clean probes of BH physics, as all of their properties are determined from
perturbation theory alone. This section deals with the Schwarzschild case, while Kerr
QQNMs are described in Section 4.4 below.
4.3.1. Quadratic modes in equatorial symmetry
Let us expand around the Schwarzschild
metric ¯gµν to second order in a bookkeeping parameter ε representing the small amplitude
of the perturbations:
gµν = ¯gµν + εh(1)

(4.42)

We will start by making the simplifying assumption that the perturbations satisfy
equatorial (or reflection, or planar) symmetry. In the general case where the progenitors’
intrinsic angular momenta (“spin”) are not aligned with the orbital angular momentum,
the spins precess around the total angular momentum and this assumption is violated (see
Section 5), but for the moment let us neglect spin precession and focus on nonprecessing
binaries; this assumption will be dropped in Section 4.3.2. In this case the parity of a mode
is (−1)ℓ+m, i.e., all metric perturbations satisfy the Zerilli equation for ℓ+m even and the
Regge-Wheeler equation for ℓ+ m odd (in a more generic setting, metric perturbations
would contain both even and odd components for a fixed ℓ+m). Furthermore, equatorial
symmetry imposes a relation between the amplitudes of mirror and ordinary modes:
A(1)(−m)
ℓ(−m)n = (−1)ℓA∗(1)m
ℓmn
(4.44)
The second-order perturbations h(2)

(Regge-Wheeler) sectors, each characterized by a Regge-Wheeler or Zerilli variable Ψ±(2),
with frequency ω and angular momentum numbers (ℓ, m), using the same definitions as
at first order. By plugging the metric decomposition into Einstein’s equations, at order
ε2 one obtains the equations of motion of Ψ±(2),
d2Ψ±(2)

QNMs, the general case being obtained by superposition. Three essential properties of
the quadratic source term S can be derived by squaring the linear QNMs in Eq. (4.43):
(1) It contains a sum of terms oscillating at frequencies ω1 + ω2, where ωi are the
frequencies of the two linear modes 1 and 2 entering the square. In particular, since
regular modes can couple to mirror modes, the real parts of the QNM frequencies
can be added or subtracted, while the imaginary parts always add.

will be proportional to a 3j symbol

In fact, the only dependence of the source term on m, m1 and m2 is fully contained
in the 3j symbol because of angular momentum conservation.
(3) The amplitude of the source is quadratic in the linear mode amplitudes A(1).
This nonlinear source term was computed in early works [755, 773–775] for the case
ℓ= ℓ1 = ℓ2 = 2, and in [757, 758] for ℓ= 4, ℓ1 = ℓ2 = 2. The generic ℓ, ℓ1, ℓ2 case was
tackled in [756, 776, 777], although only in recent work [768, 769] expressions for the
source term became publicly available (see Appendix A). In the most general case, the
source for a single product of two linear modes has the following form:
S = F1(r)Ψ(1)
1 Ψ(1)
2
+ F2(r)(Ψ(1)
